REGISTERED FOR TRANSMISSION ABROAD

30,990. (2nd April, 1894). Investing Rural Sanitary Authority with Urban Powers: Determining " Special Expenses." RUNCORN UNION. Frodsham Township, Halton, Contributory Places. and Weston _

TO THR GUARD.. -33 POOR of tho Runcorn Union, in the County of Chester, being the Sanitary Authority for the Rural Sanitary District of that Union :

And—t O-all-others whom it may concern. WHERNAS by certain orders dated respectively the 22nd day of October, 1881, the 17th day of November. 1883, the 10th day of February, 1888. the 17th day of October, 1892, the 10th day of October, 1893, and the 4th day of January, 1894, issued in purauance of the Public Health Act, 1875, and of the Public Health Acts Amendment Act, 1890, W. the Local Government Bobrd, invested the Guardians of the Poor of the said Runcorn Union, in such Sanitary Authority as aforesaid, with certain of the powers, rights, duties, capacities, liabilities, and obligations of an Urban Sanitary Authority as regards certain portions of their District, including the Contributory places of Frodsham Township. Halton, and Weston. And whereas we have received and duly considered an application under Section 276 of the Public Health Act, 1875, from the said Sanitary Authority to put in force within the said contributory places certain provisions of such Acts as hereinafter mentioned. NOW THEREFORE, in pursuance of the powers given to us by the Statutes on that behalf, we hereby declare and order as follows : Artiste 1. Until we by Order otherwise direct, the respective provisions of the said Public Health Act, 1875, hereinatter mentioned shall be in force within the Contribu- tory place or places in respect of which they are mentioned, and the said Sanitary Authority shall be invested, as regards the said Contributory places, with all the powers, rights, duties, capaci lea, liabilities, and obligations of an Urban Sanitary Authority under such respective provisions, namely,— Within the Contributory Place of Frodsham Township,— Sections 39 and 66. Within the Contributory places of Halton and Weston, —The provisOns of the Towns Improvement Clauses Act, 1847. with respect to slaughter hou:Tes, incor- porated with the Public Health Act, 1875, by section IE9 (paragraph 2) ; section 169 (paragraph 3) ; and section 170. _ _

Article 2. The expenses incurred or payable by the said Sanitary Authority in the execution of the powers conferred upon them by Article 1 of this order, except so far as such expenses may relate to the establishment and Officers of the said Sanitary Authority, shall be deemed to be "Special Expenses" within the meaning of the Public Health Act, 1875, and shall be charged as such upon the Contributory place in respect of which they may have ben incurred. Article 3. This order shall come into operation on the 30th day of April. one thousand eight hundred and ninety four, and the said Sanitary Authority shall cause it to be pub- lished once in some newspaper circulated.within their District betore that date. Given under the seal of office of the Local Government Board, this Second day of April. in the year one thousand eight hundred and ninety four.

0

G. SHAW LEPEVRI3, President.

B. B. PROWS, Assistant Secretary.

"CAPITAL" CIiAT.

There is little doubt now as to the tactics which the Opposition will pursue on the Registration Bill. War to the last extremity will hardly suit their purpose, especially as on some points they may find themselves fighting against the interests of a large body of people whom the Government may succeed in enfranchising before the general election. They will therefore attack the bill not in the main prin- ciple but by a lateral movement, ard they will at tLe same time offer terms. lithe Government will consent to drop the plural voting clauses and to abandon the hsrd-and-last proposal for holding the elections on a Saturday—provisions which, it will be contended, have nothing to do with regis- tration—the purely registration provisions .are to receive a friendly consideration. That is to say, they are to be debated in Committee with due bre- vity,and the bill in the foim in which it passes the C.,mmons is to be safe for a second reading at all. evtnts in the Upper House. It is easy to see that thso Opposition will take their stand on much more ressonable ground if they adopt this plan than it they refuse even to consider the measure. On the other hand, it is needless to say that if the provi- sions referred to are abandoned the measure will Jose 1 four-fifths of its value in Lile eyes of the Liberal party, and I belie% e that nine out of ten of the st.p- porters or the Government would prefer to see the bill wrecked by the House of Lords than carried through in an emasculated form.

If it be true that Baron Hirsch recently enter- tained at the Savoy Hotel sixty guests who were worth an average a million each, it is just as well that the festivity (Ihl not take place 011 Budget night, otherwise the Chancellor of the Exchequer's proposed death duty of seven or eight per cent. on their possessions might not have been quite condu- cive to Komi appetite or healthy die.-stion. St iil, men who pay with a light heart,Xl,2oo for a single unifier may possibly bear with complacency the effect of heavy death duties on their heirs.

In any speculations that are made at present as to the prospects of the Government, heed must of course be paid to the threatened attitude of .the more extreme Welsh members, led by Mr. Lloyd George. It would appear from Mr. Cleorge's speech din Saturday that unless the Welsh Disestablishtnent Bill comes before the Evicted Tenants Bill and- the Scotch Grand Commiltre he intends to lend no as- sistance to the tiovernment. With regard to the Evicted Tenants Bill, nothing need be said; but politicians here are disposed to regard Mr. George's view as somewhat unreasonable, so tar the zcolch Committee is concerned. Only a few hours more art requirei for this proposal to be carried, and the effects of its adoption will, of course, be to make the way clearer than before for the Wei6ll Bills. Mr. George is personally very popular with all sec- tions of Mtuisterialists, and too much confidence is felt in his juogment to enable people to believe that he would be a party to destro3ing a Government pledged to carry through the very reform for whicit is is pleading. The latest rumour is that a Welsh Disestablishment Bill will be dealt with during the Hut umni ses&ion, Or ti at it will have the first place in the Ministerial programme fcr next year.

On all sides the news of the Attorney General's prt,motion to be Lord of Appeal in Ordinary has been receiv..d with great satisfaction. There is, it is ti ue, a feeling among Chancery barristers that it, would have been well if Lord Bowen's successor had been an equity lawyer. But the signal claims of Sir Charles Russell, both personally and politi- cally, to a seat in the supreme tribunal are ad- mitted on all hands ; and no doubt is felt by those who knew him and have practised before him in patent ewes that he will makr an extremely strong judge. Moreover, it is understood that in the et exit of the Lord Chief Justiceship becoming vacant while the present Government is in ffice Sir Charles Russell will accept the post, and will thus fin.t the judicial sphere for which he is perhops best fl ; ted. Sir John Rigby moves up to the Attorney Ueneralship, tan! I learn that Mr. R. T. Reid, Q C., will be appointed Solicitor General.

Mr. Watkins, of Piccadilly, has on•view a remark- ably fine collection of butteiflies, which compri,es many buttedlies rarely seen in the very best museums. Among them is a deep blue butterfly from the Solomon blends, not obtained without. the loss of human life. The first naturalists who attempted to find it were eaten by the natives, together with their spoil. Then there is a lovely buttetfly from a small island near New Guinea, Which is believed to be a nearly if not quite extinct species. More familiar butterflies are those which mimic leaves, and these are well represented. The queen of butteifLes, as it is called, may also be seen. It c,.tnes from Columbia, and is so dazzling in the radiancy of its colouring that it can hardly be look at in bright sunlight. An extremely rare butterfly is brought from the Gahoon river. A single specimen of thb butterfly cost a late collec- tor .E5OO, tve expenses incurred by a special colh•c- tor, who took two years to find it. Only two specimens, almost priceless in value, are known of the female of this buttei fly. The mothsare equally intert,sting in Mr. IVatKin's collection, and it certainly ought to be examined by any entomo- logist.

The past few days have considerably altered the a-pect of affairs in the real estate market. His- torical estates promise to make a record show. Such celebrated properties as Rodborough Manor, Glou- cestershire ; Cornbury, the, Oxfordshire seat of Lord Churchill ; Dud Clarendon, Wiltshire, whose his- torical interest dates back to tt.ear 1164, when the well-known Constitutions of Clarendon were 51211 ed, have come into the market. Su far, L)rd Ashburton's example in having his extensive ,Somersetshire estates offered in lots, so as to give his tenants an opportunity of acquiring the free- hold of their holdings, does not seem to have found many imitators. -

The Royal Botanical Society held its exhibition of spring flowers on Wedne:day in Regent's Park. he wew her was dull arid threatening, but never-' thelesS an immense crowd of fashionable people wade it difficult to move through the conservatories. Outside the band of the Ist Life Guards entertained the company with music. The great masses of colour were chiefly furnished by the azalias, which were in extraordinary beauty; and the roses were as lovely as they could be in June. A magnificent yellow alum was not only a curicsity . but also attracted universal admiration. The greatest curi- osity of the show was a jet, black iris—at least it was so nearly black that it was only on the closest examination that any tingle of colour could be seen. But experiments of this kind in floriculture cannot be commendee. We want beautiful flowers and not queer flowers, 'unless, indeed, they are • naturally queer, like pitcher plants.

Mr. Charles P. Smith, the secretary of the Guild- hall School of Music, who died on Wednesday, had held his position from the time the school was founded by the Corporation of London in 1880, when less than one hundred pupils were accom- modate:A in a disused warelnimse in Aldermanbury. It was largely due to his exertions and his clever business management that the institution grew to such proportions as to necessitate the t-rection of the present building on the Victoria Embankment, where the students now number close upon 3,500. On the death of the late Principal, he virtually undertook the direction of the school until to appointment of Sir Joseph Barnby. He was a general favourite with professors and pupils alike.

A STRANGE WANDERER.

Moscow has just been visited by Antony the Wan- derer,' a pilgrim who strolls about the Russian dominions laden-with fifty pounds weight of iron cl,ains, and nn iron belt weighing another thirty pounds. Thus equipped, and barefoot—for, having given his boots and most of his clothes to a beggar thirty years ago, he made a vow to go barefoot thenceforth—and accQmpanied by a Sister of lititercy, who gave up wealth to join his pil- grimages, he has tramped all over the Ce; rwasus,Siberia, Central ASia, and Mongolia ; and now lw has come to Moscow to sun,rintend the casting ot a big bell tor Cronstadt Cathedral. He collects enormous sums of money, and devotes them t building churches and schools where there are none, and giving splendid pre- sents to existing churches.

There is a woman in Sitka known as Princess Tom, who is very rich. She at one time had three husbands, but has becobne Christianised, and has discharged two. She is an extensive trader, is known all over Alaska.and wears upon her arm thirty gold bracelets, made out of twents-dollar 2old pieces. Mr. °vow Wade, the sculptor, says that to make one perfect pair of arms for his Aphrodite he had to take five snodek, and select the best points in the arms of each to make up a composite model. The most grace- fully-roundea arms, Mr. Wade says, are to be found in working women—i.e., women Who do household work. The 'Comte de Montesqureu, a rich and eccentric Frencb nobleman, lives in the neighbourhood of Paris in a show place, especially remarkable for the dining-room, which contains a winter-garden. Walkimg about among the plants are a great number of small live Venetian turtles, Variously enanrilled, and encrusted with preci- ous stones.

Lady Colin Campbell lives permanently in London where, at Carlisle Mansions, she lives in au atmospherit of art and luxury. She is a highly-cultivated woman, with a passion for pictures, and is herself a competent critic of a canvas. Possessed of a fine presence and a sweet voice, she makes an attractive lecturer, and creates ai, impression at all her public appearances.- Her house at Carlisle Mansions is what a society patagraphist would term a "dream," with its artistic internal ar- rangements and bric-a-brac.

A Lady Novelist. Miss Beatrice Herraden, the authoress of the now famous Snips that Pass iii the Night." was born says the " Gentlewoman" in 1864. Miss Harraden received the fint part of her education at-Dresden, arid afterwards took aB A. degree in classics and meth ciliates at the London Uni- versity. Miss Harraden dislikes the typewriter. Education of Girls.

The great. defect in most schools for gir's is the utter neglect of phrtical education; whereas it should have prior claim to their intellectual education, if there NZ$ any difference made at all. 14 e need, says "Cassell' 80.. k of Health," strong healthy, vigorous women, and not fragile, fainting, insipid creatures; and yet no attempt is made to produce them during the only years in which they can be produced—viz., their active years of growth and development ; their exercise usually consists only of a stately walk in the street, weather permitting; dancing calisthenics, and deportment once a week; sometimes lawn tennis. Such neglect should not be. It seems to me the very way to produce girls who are useless as companions, wives who live on a sofa. and mothers who are unfit for tot-4r duties. Besides, if greater pains were ta,ken to make vigorous bodies, we should have more vigorous brains, so that a double gain would result. Another pint is the neglect, as a rule— happily not without exceptions—of the morning cold bath for girls ; and yet there is nothing that so tends to create health awl strength, to say nothing of ch anliness. What is provided instead ? A warm bath once a week. It must not be forgotten that girls are naturally rn r Subject than boys to nurvous excitement; which, could be kept more under control by a better physical develop- ment. Because of teis nervous condition, I think com- peti ive examinattons for girls, as a ruled are bad. Witness the terrible breakdoans the physician so often sees resulting. If this age be one in which young people cannot live unless they are-competing,, let us err on the right side, and let our English girls be taught to excel in very womanly exercise, in preference to nervous excitemeet and a preternatural devel)pment of the emotions. But why are our girls what they are? Some of them nothing but nerves, others nothing but emo- tii ins. others ready to faint on any or without any pro- v, cation. they are simply suffering from thsir faulty eciunation, their narrow mode of lite, and their prepos- ter ,us nineteenth century dress. We want more muscles and less nerves in our girls, it we are to have them healthy and vigorous.

It was still early evening when the Lady Bea- trice, in her humble disguibe, slowly approached Hampton House. What would he say to our gentle, lovely Master Fay ? •' "Bush, Mary!" said the Lady Beatrice. "Do not bre,the those names within these- walls ! I tremble let one evil wind should catch the sound and beat it to my. father's ears." _ _

She clasped the hand, of her faithful servitress, an I then turned to quit the rooms. "Stay in my rooms while I am cone. Marv." she stirl, pausing at the threshold. "Keep the doors lock e, , as usual, until my return." She too', her opera clock and hood on her arm an,l qui the apartment, descending the stairs to the di awing-room. Lord flampton, in full evening dress, sat alone by the pleasant hearth. He was the picture of gkoin and desolation, looking like one upon whom a. great trouble was pressing heavily. He started up, his countenance lightening as his . du uz;hter swept into his presence. Even in his anger and grief he could not but re- mark to himself how royally beautiful she was. No fairer vision ever greeted a proud father's gaze. Superb, haughty, and impassive, with a rare dignity. that would have befitted 'a queen, there was nothing overbearing or supercillious in the Lady Beatree. While her heart seemed as cold as Arctic snows, her glorious eyes beamed with a• keen and active intelligence, and her haughtiness was 'tempered with a sweet courtesy, which was as grateful as a soft south wind in winter. With her queenly attire setting off her glittering beauty, the Lady lieatrice was m magnificent representation of an ideal Cleopatra. It seemed impossible, looking at her now, that those st!a riot lips should ever have been curved, in surny smiles, that those radiant eyes should ever have glowed with tenderness and love. It seemed impossible that she could be the home angel of l'he Evergreens. She inclined her head in a s'ately courtesy to her gra y- haii ed father, who returned her salutation gravely, even coldly. _ ._ _

"Sit 'down, Beat-rice," he said, indicating a chair near his own. "You are dressed for Lady Der- vent's, I see. We will not need to go for three hours yet. We will have a frank talk together." The Ludy Beatrice tossed her white opera wrap- plugs up ,n a sofa, and accepted the proffered chair. ‘Vell, my father," she said, after a pause, as the earl resumed his seat and sank into silence. "You wished to speak to me of Lord Adlowe, ?"

. . no, Beatrice; it is of yourself I would !.. cried the earl, impetuously. "I talked with you last evening, but you put me off with idle words. You are living a monstrous mystery under my own eyes! What does it all mean? As your Lai, er, I command you to answer me 1" The Lady Beatrice feigned a cold surprise. "You sp,nk in riddh-s, papa," she said, quietly. " u understand me, Beatrice. You know wli at I mean. Last night a cry of fire was raised. [he smoke issued from your chamber. Adlowe and .1 burst in your door. I expected to find you drugged with opium, or—or worse!" and the earl's passionate tones trembled, and his pale face Unshed. "But the reality was worse than my anticipations. I found you—gone P' "Well, what of that, papa ?" asked the Lady Beatrice, speaking likely. You speak as if my absence had been a frightful calamity." The earl looked at her searchingly. " You do not see what a revelation it was to me, Beatrice," he said, striving to speak more calmly. " I fund your jewels heaped together, your dress on your closet floor. I knew that, in your absence of lalt night, I had learned the secret you have so jealously guarded all these years. Your mysterious seclusions have been simply the cover of similar absences. While I have thought of you as possibly the prey of a debasing habit, you have been away from the shelter of my roof—l know not where ; I know not with whom. This mystery is appalling —is disgraceful !" "Hush, father I" said the Lady Beatrice, with an imperious gesture. "Do not, link the word disgrace with the name Beatrice Hampton I" Her kindling cheeks and, flashing eyes warned the earl that such a coupling would be an insult undeserved by his daughter. Puzzled and bewildered, he wrung his hands helplessly. "What am I to think ?!' he (tried, his anger yielding to grief. "I know I have never been a demonstrative father, Beatrice; but I have loved you as few fathers love their children. I never taught you to confide in me. You lost your mother early. The') came your cruel lesson learned from that villian Geoffrey Trevelyan, whose ashes I could curse when I look upon you.. Beatrice, have you no heart? Does my anguish stir no chord in your soul? I pray Heaven that, should you ever marry, and should children be born to you, that they may love you more than my child loves me." _ _

The Lady Beatrice turned he face beyond the power of his tearful gaze. Her statuesque figure drooped a little. " Father " she said, in a tone softer than he had ever heard her employ before, "as you say, you were never a demonstrative father. You -never used to caress me or tell me that you loved me. I knew that you were 'proud of me. I loved you, father. I love you now, very dearly, very. ten- derly. But the affair you spoke of just now seemed to have changed my whole nature. I loved Geoffrey Trevelyan. Our betrothal had your heatry appro- bation. You desired more than all things else to link the fortunes of Hampton and Trevelyan. Had Geoffrey not died in South America, but lived to return to England, you might to-day have seen your daughter a happy wife, and have gathered your grandchildren around your knees." "Never 1" said the earl, violently. "I would recognise no grandchild in whose veins flowed the blood of that midnight assassin and robber, Geoffrey Trevelyan. Not even if Lord Trevelyan had forgiven his vile nephew would I have trusted your happiness to him, or linked our proud name to his infamous one. Thank heaven, he died, Beatrice 1 I heard Lord Trevelyan say, not long since, that if his degenerate nephew, by some strange Providence; still existed, as Lord Adlowe has once or twice fancied possible, he would employ every detective on the police force to hunt(llirn out and bring him to a rightful punishment. I declare to you, Beatrice' I sym- pathise with the miserly marquis in that senti- ment. I womd assist him in as search for Geoffrey Trevelyan should he chance to live. But this is wild talk," he added. "Happily, Geoffrey is dead."

"Yet you loved Geoffrey once," said the Lady Beatrice, in a subdued One. "One would think, father, that Lord Trevelyan would want to screen iieoffrey, instead of delivering him up to the law. Geoffrey was his brother's son, his own heir-ap- parent, the bearer of his mime. Geoffrey's disgrace would be a family disgrace." "No one would b:arne the uncle for his nephew's crime," said the earl briefly. "The marqui6 rightly loathes his nephew. I consider that, if Geoffrey had lived Lord Trevelyan would have been justified in any act to prevent Geoffrey's in heriting his titles and fortunes. The disgrace of his living would be worse than any disgrace oi merited punishment." " It is as well thftt Geoffrey is dead,' said Lady fiet4ritict, with a alsvca-, "-11441. 11%;

L.., Leturn, you would have h low op to his enemies, had they failed to discover him. Poor Geoffrey! I loved him, papa. You enct.ur- aged that love. You turned from him in his troubles. My heart is with him now. My poor Geoffucy ! " " We will talk no more of him," said the earl. "It is strange how in our private talks thC name of Geoffrey Trevclyan always creeps in. . The fortunes of Treyelyan and Hampton will be united when you marry Lord Adiowe. And that brings us back to my discovery of last evening. "Father," she said, calmly, after a long silence, "you do not act the part of a wise man in judging of my seclusions by my absence of last evening. Because I was absent once, it does not follow that am always absent when my doors are locked, I confess that I was out last evening, but you should have had sufficient confidence in y6ur daughter to believe and trust in her honour and wisdom. lam no child, that my goings and comings should 'be questioned." "But the mystery, Beatrice ?" cried her father, in a tone of anguish. "Since you force me%to confess my where- abouts.," exclaimed the Lady Beatrice, a scarlet dow burning on either white cheek, "I will tell you. I weut where I have been before," and her proud voice faltered, 'to visit an obscure family to whom my presence brinzs sunlight and happiness. Shall I give you a dst of my charities, father?"

rhe gloom cleared from the earl's face, and all traces of his an.er vanished. "No, no, Beatrice," he said, heartily. "You never told a falsehood in your life. I would stake my honour on your truthfulness. I believe that last night you visited some poor people. But why were you gone all night? Why were you absent all day ? ' _

"I -stopped with friends." "I did not see you come in, Beatrice. I have watched for you all day," said the earl, gravely. "You still dititrust me, then? Be it so, father," cried the Lady Beatrice, a passionate .tremor un clerlying her cold tones, as tire might underlie ice. "If I sin to give an account of my every action, I may as well sink into my dotage at once. If one is not competent to be trusted with 'one's self at four and thirty, one had better be in an asylum for imbeciles."

E XA NEIV TITPDA I, APRIL 21, 1894.

HOW THE "CAT" IS ADMINISTERED.

A tit ARRINGTON ROUGH UNDER LASH.

The closing day at the recent criminal assizes held in Liverpool, says a contemporary of,. that town, was one to which a number o( prisomer4 con- victed of robbery with violence looked toi ward with consi.lerable anxiety, inasmuch as it was the nate on which Mr. Justice Day had decided to pass sentence on the highway robbers who had been found guilty during the sitting of the court. It was known to those offenderti that his lordship invari- ably ordered such prisoners to be flogged with the "ca,' in addition to receiving the punishment of short terms of imprisonment with hard labour. A little before five o'clook on the afternoon of Easter Monday—a day, which will now be memorable to nine young men at present in Walton Geal—the batch of culprits was placed in the doek in St. Georges Hall to hear the decision of the court, and a little over a fortmight has only elapsed before they experienced the painful reality of the solitence paased upon them, for last week they received tieo first ins, alment of their ponishment. One of the prisoners hails from Warrington, two from Bootle, one from Wiry, and flit() from Liverpool, and, according to the gravity of the off rice of which they were found guilty, were they ordered to receive lasbes from the "cat o' nine tails." In passing sentence his lordship in one or two instances said that prisoner must go to goal tor a short period and be publically fl 'geed with the "ca'." The use of' the word " puolically " was evi- d oitly an inadvertency, for the days of public flogging are gone. It is generally brlteved, however, that the offsriders have their punishment administered in the pr sence of other prisoners ; but this, we are informed, is ta-ely, if ever, done now. The batch of highway robbers who were flogged a day or two ago at Walton Pri,mn were not, however, subj4cted to the additional pain of being whipped before any of their comrades in distress, although their howls were distinctly audible to many who were Inc ircerated in the goal. Some of the offenders bore the strokes from the " cat " with tracea that bravado which they exhibited when the Sentence was pronounced, whilrt others cringed and screamed in a manner whieh must have remindeel the officials who wi.nessed the flogging of his lordship's words--" Some of these ruffians have no inanliiiess in /hem." It is erniecessary, however, here to dwell upon the sufferings of the men who must still be malting under the strokes of tive " cat" as administered by one of the warders who;le duty it was to carry out he sentence. Of course, prior to the fligging, the doctor examined the prisoners as to their plhsical condition and titeess to undergo the ponishment, and, according to rezulations, the order Of voltrt. was earned out in the presence of the medical officer and of the governor, of the prison.

SUPPER AND THEN SLEEP.

When after a hard days' work, a man comes home and iays " Wife, lam very tired." she isn't a bit surprised He has a right to be tired. He has earned his supper and his sluep. But after he has had a good, plentiful feed, what if he should saF. " How sorry I am I ate my supper Th3n on getting up in the morning, suppose be began ta growl in this style : " What a fool I was to go to bed ; in the mornin; I feel M )ra tired than I do at night."

On hearing this sort of talk his wife would think he was getting out of his head, or that some complaint had seized on him. And she would be Tiite right. Yet plenty of people take this apparently absurd atti- tude; men and women both; They get at odds with things ; blessing turns t bane, light to darkness, and nature's b wnties become Ours es ,like singing birds trans- formed to bats.

" I was always tired and languid," writes a woman, awl ached from head to foot.

This is an instance of what we say. It is unfortunate and sometimes even wrong. To be . always tired and languid is out of nature. Flowers themselves seldom wilt an 1 droop until autumn comes. Slam other things she says which Inv help to explain " All my life." her letter runs, "I have been weak awl When I was sixteen, it was thought I had con- sumption. My appetite was very poor, and after eating

I gre it pain and tightness across the chest, au! wool! swell so much I was obliged to loose my clot. ing." This at sixteen I An age when youth should inhabit a heaven of its own, full of strength, quick impulses, activity, bright fancies, without a paia or an ache. But come, we forget the children's legacies. Often when parents oin lesve no money they transpit disease. More shame to them.

Well. we must get on. The letter continues "Fre- quently I had so much pain I coul I scarcely draw breath for an hoar. I often threw myself on the couch wonder- ing if I cculd live much longer in this way. I grew to be afraid to eat, as all food alike disagreed with me." What a dreadful thing! It is the same as being choked by pure air. Such a condition is nothing less than diabolical. It suggests the idea of being murdered by one's own best friend. .

"I was also much troubled," says the lady, "with sickness, as though something should come up, and sointimes threw up. phlegm streaked with blood. I suffered from constant pain and pressure at the heart, which I believed to be heart disease. It was rare that I ha i a good night's rest, and often awoke with a sense of smothering. I was so bad that sometimes my husband had to knock my back to get my breath into me.

"After a time a short dry cough fixed upon me, and gradually I got so weak that everybody thought I was in a decline and wasting away. I was just able to drag nayselt about, and often had to stop and rest. Mv life was a misery, and it was a trouble to live, yet thus it went on year after year. For 16 or 17 years, time and time, I was under the doctors, but wa,s no better for all thvir medicines.

"In September, 1885, my hushan I begged ml to try Mother Seigel's Curative Syrup, and to please him I did so. After I had used one bottle I felt' so much better I was glad to buy another myself. Soon my food diges- ted, and all pain left me. My appetite returned, I gained strength. and I have never been se well in my life as I have been for the past seven years. I tell every- one what the Syrup has done for me, and willingly c sent to the publication of this letter. Yours truly, (Mrs.) Einbeth Hope—wife of Mr. Andrew Hope, builder—. Mss-lane, Styal, Handforth, Cheshire, June 24th, 1892."

In this climate lung complaints are lamentably com- mon, but not nearly so common as people think. In the opinion of her friends (and d ctors too, nernaps) Mrs. Hope had organic lung disease, and must die. Yet her quick restoration to health, by the use of Mother Seigel's Curative Syrup, proves her complaint to have been indigestion and dyspepsia—an ailment practically universal, and in the end more fatal than consumption, which is one of its results when long continued. What is the lesson? Very simple. Cure the cause.

Thus you may eat well, sleep soundly, work to advan- :age and happily live out-all your allotted days:

VISIT TO. THE RUNCORN LIBERAL CLUB. Mr. J. T. Brunner, M.P. for the division, previous to leaving Runcorn on Satut day evening, -paid a visit to the Liberal Club. Ile was accompanied by Messrs. S.Taylor, J.P:, J. Clarke, liniveton,W.Band- ! I ' ley T. Handley, T. Riley, A. R. Norman, Hppe, Pas- nett,and other gentlemen. Mr. Clarke occupied the chair, and after a brief address called upon Mr. BRONNKR, who was received with enthusiastic cheering. After explaining that he should have reached the club earlier but the hospitab!e house of Mr. Taylor proved too great an attraction for him, he expressed his glatifieation to the members of the club for having in- vited him to visit them. He was struck by the com- fort. jovialitv,and prosperity displayed inside the build- ing when he entered it. He wou:d, however, warn 'them against over-prosperity, as he had been impressed by an old saying of the late Mr. John Bright that political clubs were rather a danger to a party than a support, especi- ally for the Liberal party, because when a club be- came respectable,the less Liberal it would be. He there- fore warned them against prosperity and respectability, and asked them when they had paid off the debt on the club,which they were in process of doing,not to "black- ball" the young Liberals, but give them a hsarty wel- come. (Loud 'applause.) They had had exciting times since they were last face to face. They bad lost THE GREATEST LEADER. THE LIBERAL PARTY EVER HAD. He might quote the words so giacieu-ly, nobly, and worthily uttered by his opponent the Marquis of Salis- bury who said that "The greatest figure in English Parliamentary history had now passed away." Those worJs he appreciated to the full. They must remem- her that Mr. Gladstone was no longer leading, them, but they must never forget the advice and encourage - meet he gave them in the last speech in the House of Commons. He laid before them the duty of carrying out, and which he (Mr. Brunner) thought would be a pleasing duty to every member of that club—he bade them carry to a successful issue the struggle with the ancient. absurd, and effete second chamber of this country. FRIDAY.

In the House of Commons on Friday, Mr. MORLEY asked leave t introduce a hill to reduce the wrind of qualification for Parliamentary and local government electors to tlwe months, to provide for tha half yearly Iregistration of electors, to provide for taking the polls at a Parliamentary general election on one day, and to re- strict au elec,or ti one constituency. He said it was desired to turn into a reality the intention of Parliament that every inhabitant householder in town . and country should have his vote, so that the voice of the locality, instead of being adulterared and nullified by out-voters, should prevail. It was proposed to indicate in the bill that the polling-day should he Saturday. Mr.. BALFOUR thought a three months' qualificatirm too short. He said it would he very inconveni.nt to have every bit of com- mercial machinery stopped throughout the whole coun- try for 24 hours, and Ilk objected, too, to the cast-iron mould ot a Saturday poll. The createst inequality in the distribution of politi -al power did not, in his opinion, arise from the plural vote, but from the way in which sears were distributed. Alter a short discussion the bill was brought in and read a first time, and the second reading was fixed for the 23id inst.

FEW budgets have been anticipa- Budget. The ted with greater interest than that which was unfolded in the House of Commons on Monday evening by Sir WILLIAM HARCOURT. What are spoken of as the "secrets of the budget "had been well kept, and even the most" knowing "of London newspapers did not venture far into the region of prophecy. Those who expected a revolutionary re-arrangement of the financies of the country will be disappointed; yet, at the same time, some of the proposals w hich were made by the Chancellor of the Exche- quer are strongly indicative of the irresistible democratic tendencies of the times. The over- hauling of the death duties is, for instance, a reform which has long been asked for by progres- sive politicians. At the present time there are no fewer than five death duties—the probate,account and legacy duties, payable on personalty, and the estate and succession duties payable, though not in the same degree, on both personalty and realty It is now proposed to carry out the much-needed simplification of these imposts. The probate, account, and estate duties will be abolished, and their place will be taken by a single impost,known as the estate duty, which will be charged on the principal value of all property, real or personal, settled or unsettled, without regard to the heir or legatees, degree of relationship to the dead. There is also to be an amalgamated legacy and succession duty. This simplification of the law in regard to these duties is well worth having for its own sake, but, in addition, Sir WILLIAM HARCOURT has taken the opportunity to adopt the highly equitable principle of graduation. He now proposes that while properties of under .-C3OO pay death duties of one per cent., the charge shall gradually rise to eight per cent, on properties exceeding a million. This is a recognition of the principle of graduation which will delight all financial reformers. Though the present proposal is an exceediagly moderate and tentative applica- tion of the principle, it is the thin end of the wedge. We may be sure that in years to come the principle will be much more widely applied, and there will be a steady transference of the weight of taxation from the shoulders of the poor or the comparatively poor to those of the wealthy. The present budget does not go beyond suggesting a death duty of eight per cent. on bloated fortunes of a million and upwards, but now that the new path has been entered upon, it requires no great gift of prescience to foresee the day when million- aires will not escape so easily. In regard, too, to I the income tax, Sir WILLIAM HARCOURT propose) to benefit • those with smaller incomes at the expense of those who can better afford to pay. There will be a penny added to the tax, but all whose income falls helow £l6O a year will be entirely exempt. At the present time the man with £l5O a year has to pay on £3O of his income,but in future such a man will be altogether free from that tax. There will be an abate- ment of £l6O for salaries up to £4OO, and of £lOO for salaries reaching £5OO. It will be seen, there- fore, that in dealing with death duties or income tax, Sir WILLIAM HARCOURT proceeds on thoroughly democratic lines. There will, per- haps, be greater differences of opinion as to the increase of sixpence on every gallon of spirits and barrel of beer, but in having recourse to these sources of revenue the Chancellor of the Exchequer is undoubtedly following the prece- dents of many years past.

IT is pretty generally conceded The Doom of that there are two methods of Coercion. governing Ireland—one by coer- cive laws and the othor by a generous extension of self-government, or Home Rule. The Liberals advocate li@me Rule, and the Tories are in favour of coercion—at least they were supposed to be in favour of coercion until Wednesday, but on that day there was a rather significant incident in the House of Commons. Colonel NOLAN, one of the Irish members, moved the second reading of a Bill for the repeal of the Irish Coercion Act. This Coercion Act was passed seven years ago and its provisions were freely used by the Tories when in office. When the Liberals came into power the Act was suspended, Ireland having since been governed entirely under the ordinary law. This piece of coercive legislation still, however, remained on the statute book ; hence Colonel NOLANS proposal for its repeal. It might have been supposed that the Tories would have eagerly seized upon this oppor- tunity for the reiteration of their understood opinions as to the proper way of governing Irish- men, and as a matter of fact it was expected that they would offer the m ost strenuous opposition to the proposal for the repeal of the Coercion Act. But even Tories in these days cannot aftord to linger too far behind the times, and it seems to be dawning upon them that coercion as a method of government under rep resentative institutions is played out. The opposition they gave to Colonel NOLAN'S proposal was—always excepting of course the Ulster wing—singularly slack and half-hearted notwithstanding the drumming up of the Party whips, and the Bill was read a second time with a swinging majority of sixty. When Tories lose faith in coercion we are surely on the eve of Home Rule.

LOCAL BRA.NCISES OF THE CARPENTERS AND

JOINERS SOCIETY.

The annual report in connection with the Amal- gamated Society of Carpenters and Joiners has been issued. The Society now includes no less than 629 branches and 40.996 members. The total income amounted to £109,722 12s. Bd., being an increase of about £10,898 13s. over the revenue of 1892. The expenditure has been £109,441 13s. lold. or £18,596 138. 11d, in excess of the previous year. Notwith- standing this increased expenditure the cash balance was increased frGm £72,639 12s. 50. to £72,920 11s. 3d. Generally speaking the society is in a very flourishing condition. In the Warrington branch there are 116 members from wh9m there was a total income of £302 ss. 2d. during the year, as well as a balance in hand of .C3OO 13s. 61. The expenditure during the year amounted to £364 Bs. 10d. leaving a balance in hand of £238 9s. 10d. In the Widnes branch there are 65 members and the income for the year was £261 183. ; there is a balance in hand of £l2l 3s. Bd. The Runcorn branch numbers 34 members with a total income of £lO5 58. id. and a balance in band of £76 13s. 7d.

MARRIAGE OF MISS SEFTON OF RUNCORN.

Considerable interest was manifested in the marriage of Miss Sarah Ann Sefton, only daughter of the late Mr. Henry Sefton, contractor, to Mr. Robert Yates, eldest son of Captain William Yates, which took place on Wednesday afternoon at the Halton-road Wesleyan Church. The Rev. James Hanby performed the cere- mony. The bride was attended by two bridesmaids namely Misses Hilda Hewitt, Tuebrook ; and Mary Emma Jellicoe, Lower Bebineton. Mr. George Yates, the bridegroom's brother acted as best man. The bride was given away by her grandfather, Mr. Jellicoe, far- mer, of Bebington. After the ceremony, the party breakfasted at the house of the bride, and subsequently the newly wadded pair travelled by train to North Wales where the honeymoon will be spent. Many costly presents have been received.

RUNCORN FRER LIBRARY COMMITTER.

The usual monthly meeting of the Runcorn Free Library Committee took place on Tuesday evening at the Town Hall, Runcorn, Mr. W. Hankinson presiding. There were also present : Messrs. S. Pepe and T. Riley. Mr. J. D. Jones, librarian, submitted the annual and monthly financial reports. The former shows that at the 25th March, 1893, there was a balance in hand of £2O 24. 9d. ; receipts during the year amounted to £222 19s. id., showing the total to be £243 is. Md. The payments for the same period were : Salaries, £6O; new books, £76 1.1.5. 2d.; magazines and newspapers, £24 7s. id. ; which with other smaller sums brought the total up to £237 Os. 3d. ; thus leaving a balance in hand on the 25th March last of £6 is. 7d. The number of books issued during the month from the 25th March to April 17th was 2,080 made up as follows :—Theology 47, philosophy 7, science 117, history, &1., 168, poetry 22, fiction 1,511, miscellaneous 161. These were out of the lending department, whilst 47 volumes under the heading of miscellaneous were issued from the reference department. The increaso on the corresponding month of last year was 240 in the lending department, and 2 in the reference department. The statement of accounts and also the report were adopted. Mr. Jones also reported the following gifts to the Library during the month :—Presented by the author, Mr. 11. C. Burdett, Prince, Princess and People" ' • by the Treasurer of the Religious Tract Society, "'rho Annotated Paragraph Bible"; and "Library reports from Birmingham and Liverpool."

THE BOWLING SEASON AT RUNCORN

On Wednesday the opening game of bowls of the Run- corn Bowling Club took place, the teams being picked by the president and vice-president. The encounter proved very interesting and ended in an easy victory for the president's team by 103 points to 77. Tea was then partaken of and afterwards the main of bowls handicap was played. There were sixteen entries, one limit being three points. This was won by the president, the secretary taking the second by on point.

At the Widnes Police Court on Monday, before Mr. H. Wade Deacon and Alderman Timtnis, Rose Kelly was charged with committing a breach of the peace in Lugs- dale Road on Sunday morning and also with doing damage to the extent of as. to a rug, the property of the police, whilst in the cell. This was prisoner's 108th appearance and the Bench sentenced her to seven days' imprisonment for damage to the rug and on the other charge bound her over to keep the peace for two months. She was also threatened, by the Bench that if she came up again before that Court she would be sent to the Sessions as a rogue and vagabond where she would receive a much longer sentexce than could be given her in Widnes.

BIeITHESDA LITERARY AND SOCIAL CIASS, RUNCORN. _ _ .

On Tuesday evening, the last meeting of the Session of the above class was held in the Bethesda Lecture Hall, when the Rev. J. Simpson delivered an address on the poet Longfellow. The early life Of the poet was briefly described, and his first attempts at verse, which were in no wise remarkable for any extraordinrry dis- play of genius, for the poet was more noted for his plodding character than for any brilliancy of intellect. Though lacking the subtlety of Browning, with his obscure though luminous darkness, and also the intense sweetness of Tennyson, Longfellow, with his pure and simple style, had won a well-deserved popularity because of his direct appeals to the affections of home and kin- dred. At the close of the meeting, a vote of thanks was moved to Mr. Simpson and warmly confirmed by the members, for his kindness in having given thorn the series of literary addresses.

PROPOSED LICENSING OF CLUBS.

A petition from the Right Hon. Sir William Harcourt M.P. was read showing that serious evils arose from various clubs which existed throughout the union in which intoxicating Ii quors were sold to the members without any limit as to the hours during which they could be obtained. The pet itioners believed that the licensing of such clubs, and the restriction of hours for .selling intoxicants, would not only create an appreciable revenue and tend to reduce pauperism but would be a great benefit to the community at large. They, trusted that such an alteration would be effected.—The Clerk pointed out that the petition required the law to be amended so that clubs would b 2 closed at the same time as ordinary public houses.

THE GUARDIANS AND THE RWNCORN COMMISSIONERS.

The Clerk referred to the recent death of a Man named James Curzon, of Wiv ern-place, Runcorn, who at the time was suffering from typhoid fever. He (Mr. Ashton) had seen in the local papers a report of the last Commissioners' meetin4s, at which remarks were made by some of the members having reference to the above case, which, to a certain extent, reflected upon the Guardians' relievine• officer, Mr. Cooke. The latter gentleman called at Mr. Ashton's office and reported the whole case which was considered sufficiently important to be taken down in writing. He understood that the case had been referred to the Local Government Board by the Commissioners and most likely the Guardians would receive a coMmunication regarding it from the Local Government Board. Re thought therefore it was advisable to read to the Guardians the report of Mr. Cooke with reference to the matter. Mr. Ashton then read the report which was to the following effect :—On 6th March Mr. Percival, inspector of nuisances to the Commissioners, called upon Mr. Cooke in the evening and s3id that a case of typhoid fever had been reported to him by Dr. Robinson who had advised removal to the hospital. The man was named James Curzon and lived at Wivern-place. He asked Mr. Cooke what he intended to do in the matter. Having questioned him Mr. Cooke said the case was in his (Mr. Percival's) hands, and that it was for him to deal with, as he had received instruc- tions from the Guardians not to interfere with cases of infectious disease which were other than pauper cases. Also that infectious cases,which were not paupers at the time of being reported to him (Mr. Cooke) for removal, were not to be made such for the purpose of throwing certain liabilities on the Guardians which ought to be borne by the Commissioners. Later in the evening Mrs. Curzon called and tcld Mrs. Cooke that her husband was ' was very ill, suffering she thought, from typhoid fever. They were staying with her brother at Wivern Place. When questioned she told Mr. Cooke that her home bad been broken up and in coslsequence she, her husband, and two children had been living for some time with her brother. She said her brother was in work and was single. Dr. Robinson had attended her husbaud since the previous Sunday and that he had ordered his re- moval to the hospital. She had seen Mr. Percival, who had told her that she would require an order from Mr. Cooke before her husband could be removed. Mr. Cooke then told her, in reply, that he had just seen Mr. Percival, and that she needed no order from him to have her husband removed. He added to her that he thought Mr. Percival would be sure to remove her husband that night, so long as he had orders to do so. Replying to Mr. Cooke she said that Dr. Robinson's assistant had seen her husband that day. She then left the house but returned in about half-an-hour saying that she had met Mr. Percival who had sent her back and had told her that it was a medical order she required as he could not remove her husband without one. Mr. Cooke told her that was not so, and that a doctor, and everything else he required, would be provided for him at the hospital. He added that Mr. Percival had been ordered to remove him and if that was not done that night matters might be serious for him. He also ask _A her to call and tell him if he was removed by the following day, and that in the meantime Mr. Ashton should be consulted as to what should be done. He (Mr. Cooke) heard nothing more that night and was quite satisfied that the man was properly lodged, cared for, and receiving medical treatment. The next morning Mr. Ashton approved of his action in the matter, but suggested that Dr. Robin- son should be seen to satisfy himself that he was attend- ing the case. Dr. Robinson told him that Curzon had been removed to the Hospital and that evidently the man had been ill for more than a week befere the 4th of March. Continuing, Mr. Cooke said that Dr. Robin- son had not told the man's wife that he would not attend him further. He did say that the patient would be better in the hospital. On the following Wednesday Dr. Robinson told him that he could not think of attend- ing the case at the hospital unless he had other cases there. A week after removal to the hospi' al the man died, and a few days afterwards Mrs. Cutzm applied for relief which was granted. Mr. Cooke had satisfied him- self that Curzon was not destitute, but on the contrary, as Dr. Robinson was attending him, and he was living with his brother-in-law, he did not require either medical or other relief."—Mr. Heywood said that from the report of their Relieving Officer the case seemed very plain and he thought they should fully endorse the course taken by hitn.—Mr. Taylor remarked that a different case had been presented to the Commissioners by their officer and from what he had heard he thought they were quite prepared to substantiate what they had sent to the Local Government Board.—Mr. Stokes said he quite agreed with the action of their offiaer as pre- sented by the report, but he could not feel sate in expressing an opinion before the other side had been heard. —Mr. Handley was also of the opinion that the case should not be discussed before the other statement was before them.—The Chairman said the Board could, no doubt, defend itself when the crisis came.—lt was ultimately decided to leave the question in abeyance until they received any communication with reference to the matter from the Local Government Board and that in the meantime a committee should be appointed to consider the case brought against the Relieving Officer.

THE AGITATION FOR PUBLIC BATHS

AT RUNCGP N.

ENTHUSIASTIC MEETING OF RATEPAYERS. On Wednesday evening a public meeting of rate- payers was held in the Public Hall. Runcorn, to consider the advisability of constructing public baths for the town. The Chairman of tilt,. Board of Commissioners, Mr. S. Taylor, J.P., presided and was accompanied on the platform by Mr. Walter Collier, the prime mover in the question, and Mr. F. A. law clerk to the Commissioners. There was a very large attendance, and the gentlemen named, as they made their appearances on the platform, met with enthusiastic receptions.

Mr. Taylor, having explained that the meeting was convened in consequence of his having received & requisition from several ratepayers, called upon Mr. Walter Collier to open the proceedings. Mr. Collier who was received with and =Ahmed applause, said he had much pleasure in coming there that night to speak on the question of public baths. The matter had been before them for a long time,it was made the cry at every municipal election ; but he hoped that night they would take the matter up in earnest ,and vote for the provision. He contended that baths in their own town could be made self-supporting, inas- much as they would be able to get their water for nothing, and that large numbers of bathers. would pay to take advantage of them. The- site he propos2cl they should select was near to Mr. Yarwood's stables, then in the occupation of the Com- missioners, but reauy belonging to Messrs. Greenall, Whitley and Co., rewers, of Warrington: He had spoken to a direct of this firm a few days ago with reference to the la,' .1 mentioned, and the Durposa for which it might Ex. required. The director Cold him in reply that they would do their best for Ruicorn if the question of public baths was passed. He (Mr. Collier) then went on to deal at some length as to the extent to which the baths would affect the rates, and compared their own rates with those of various other towns. In 1892 the WidneA baths had produced a profit of £29 98. id. ;in 1893, £34 3s. 91. Yet the Widnes baths had been pointed our as a warning. The water was ot course pumped from certain works. He had spOken to five eminent doctors upon the subject, who all agieed that if the provision was madeathe sanitary state of the town would much improve. He had also received a letter from Mr. J. E. Worslev, the coroner for the district, who thought that baths were extremely necessary when it was considered how . Runcorn was intersected with canals, Sic. Continuing, Mr. Collier said that if the erection of baths did add a penny in the £ to the rates that was no reason why they should not have them. He then moved the following resolution :—" That this meet- ing of ratepayers feeling convinced that public baths are necessary for Runcorn urge the Runcorn Board of Commissioners to take the necessary steps for their pro- vision." (Loud applause.) Mr. Thomas Lea seconded.

Mr. Lea said that Mr. Collier had not yet told them how much the baths would cost. They were told about id. in the £ but he was rather doubtful whether a penny rate would cover the costs. kHear, hear.) Mr. Collier replied that they could have one CO by 30 feet which would cost £2,000, repayable in 30 years. If they had a first class bath towards which he felt more favourable, it would cost £3,000, the interest on which would amount to £163, being ask-A for the cost of main- taining other baths than Widnes Mr. Collier said the Warrington baths cost £193 per year.—the receipts were £lB2,—loss £11; St. Helens : cost £244, loss £BB ; show- ing that a .1-1. rate at St. Helens maintained their bath. The governing authority there was so convinced of the benefit of baths that £3,000 was to be borr.wed fdr the erection of another bath. (Applause.) Birk r:lie:,(s baths cost per year £671, receipt £491, loss £lBO ; Haaley ; cost £753, receipts £735, gain £32. Others were also co,:ted,

. Mr. Ct-.)io, after que:itioning some of Mr. Crllier'S figure!, thought a 3.1. rate ‘i as more likely t;e:cq•:irod than a 1(1. one.

Mr. Collier replied that his scheme was to get the water free therefore they would have no need t€ 4 spend money upon water. (Applause.) That being so the baths would be self-supporting. A Voice: The water you speak of has been condemned as unfit for domestic purposes, so that if you are going to swim in it and swallow any, where will the doctors be?—

Mr. Collier : The water was analysed a few days ago and pronounced "good."

Another Voice: Have we any guarantee that we shall be _able to get the water free of cost.

Mr. T. Lea answered in the affirmative.

Mr. Boyle spoke in favour of the proposition saying that a swim stimulated a man for his work, and baths afforded boys opportunities to learn to swim. lie did not think Runcorn was too heavily rated to go to the necessary expenditure and most likely in the end, the provision might possibly reduce the rates. Captain Le Couteur thought that it was very desirable that baths should be provided in Runcorn. He had often taken his boys over to Widnes for the purpose of letting them swim,and have had to pay the extra bridge fare.

Mr. Cooke said there were lots of folks in Runcorn who had bard:work to live without having to pay to get a bath. As for the Coroner he lived out of Runcorn, and there were not many medical gentlemen living in the town favourable to the proposal at the present time. Mr. Collier intimated that in some places special terms were issued for school children to bathe, so that every child would have the opportunity. There was only one place in Runcorn where a person could get a bathe if he had the misfortune ever to go there. That was at the Workhouse, and the officials would not admit a man without he had first been bathed. (Laughter and ap- plause.) _

Mr. buckett approved of the propositicl and was will- ing in company with others to contribute £5 to the scheme. He often went to Widnes to bathe, but some- times that bath was full, and on more than one occasion he had travelled to Liverpool for the purpose of having a swim.

Mr. Kniveton thought that what was wantod more immediately were public works in the neighbourhood, and they would never get them if their rates were stall increasing. He was a supporter of baths, but the pre- sent was not the time to push the question forward. The Chairman assured them there was no one more favourable to the question than himself. He had, for a long time, felt very strong on the subject, because he believed that cleanliness was the first duty of every in- dividual. (Applause.) However much he would like to see baths in Runcorn, they must remain cognizint of the fact that baths, such as Runcorn required would cost four to five thousand pounds. That amount they would have to pay back with interest in 30 years, and he pre- sumed that would amount to over 2d. in the £ of itself. Then there was the question of maintenance. Mr. Leach, at the last Board meeting had given a large amount of information on the maintenance of Laths in various parts of the country. The baths in Northwich, wh4ch they all knew were splendid ones, were given by a local gentleman who defrayed the whole cost and so made them free ; but Mr. Leach had told them that the loss to the town through maintaining them was £260 last year. Then at St. Helens he had said that each bather was a cost to the rates of sd. But as far as they were concerned ha , Runcorn he, (the chairman) was prepared to freely and willingly pay his proportion if such a provision was made. They must nbt however lose sight of the rates. Through the number of improvements that hal been effected in the town during the last year it had necessi- tated their again levying the 23. 10d. rate and although the committee had sat one night last month from seven o'clock to eleven yet they could not bring the rate down; on the contrary to keep the rate at 2e. 101 they were forced to drop some of the improvements. He would very much like to see baths in Runcorn, but he thought the present time wis inopportune. Should the meeting decide in favour of baths then he hoped the Commii- sioners would take the proposal into their favourable consideration, but he feared that when the ratepayers thought the matter over seriously they would object to to it at present.

Mr. Collier having replied, and several other expres• - sions of opinion in favour and against having ben given, the Chairman put the proposition to Lhe meeting with the result that it was carried with a large majority amid loud and prolonged cheering. Mr. T. Lea said he should certainly support the ques- tion before the Commissioners on the strength of that meeting,.—Mr. Collier after thanking the audience moved a vote of thanks to Mr. Taylor which was accorded with enthusiasm. The proceedings then terminated.

MESSRS. W. D. HOUGHTON AM) SO-NS AND THEIR. W EIGHTS. • _

• At the Warrington Cour ty Poty Sessions on Wednes- day, Mr. W. D. Houghton of the firm of Messrs. W. D. Houghtcn and Sons, Sankey Wire Mills, was charged with having on the 30th March unjust wgiights which were also unstamped —Mr. S. Wallbead represented the detendants.—lnspector Spencer stated that on Friday, the 30th. of March he visited Mr. Houghton's Wireworks, Sankey Bridges. On going into one room he found a pair of scales and a set of weights from 581bs. down- wards all unstamped. He asked the man in charge about them and he said he did not think it wa?necessary to have thPm stamped. They adjusted them AS they thought they required it. The foreman, Mr.Sinnonette, told him !hey were used for wei=hing wire as it came from the workmen, and that the worltmcn were paid for the weight of wire registered by the scale. The weights were found to be light from two drachm downwards.— In cross-examination by Mr. %%railhead, witness tt dmittPd that there was no inteution to defraud as the weights were in favour of the men.—Mr. Wallhead contended that the weights were not therefore twins% With regard to the charge of having them unstamped he suhmitted that the act was intended to prevent persons from being deceived. The weights in question were not used in trade, being merely in use in the inter-process of manu- facture. The wire was weighed again before it went to the purchaser on scales which toe Inspector had c-rtitied to be correct. He also stated that by the regulations ap- proved by the County Council, it nas the Inspector's duty to vkit the works once in twelve month, and to give the owners notice to have their scales and r weiihts used in the process of manufacture officially stamped and adjusted. This the Inspector had not done.—The Chair- man said they had decided not to convict on either of the chrges,ut hp considered that all weights used in manu- facture should be stamped.

THE LONGRIDGE MIRACLE.

AN OLD SOLDIER'S NARROW ESCAPE.

„. A soldier's story is alivays worth printing and reprint- ing, and the narrow escape which a ” Pre-ton Herald" reporter tells, from the lips of an ex-Staff Serzeant,hrars such an impress of modest heroism as to well deserve reproduction.

The narrator was Mr. R. P. Colgan, Quarry Cottage, Tootal Height, Longridge, Lanes, and his story opens with a thrilling incident which cannot be told much better than in his own words. "One day," lie said, "as an elderly woman who did washing for the officers ivas er)ssing a field with a bundle of clean liken, a tramp struulc her from behind with an iron bar. I rushed out, and halt jumped, half fell, over a wail about 16 feet high, as I pursued the man, who, however, got into a wood and escaped. In the tall I injured my back, and to that I attribute my illness.. The tramp who attacked the woman was afterwards arrested and sentenced to 18 months' imprisonment."

Very serious was the illness entailed by the worthy, Sergeant's impetuous courage. Not tne be.ttimedical care at the Military Hospital, Warwick where he was stationed, Colgan found himself hope- lessly paralysed. His limbs refused their t,ffice ; he reeled like a drunk ird, " though." he told the reporter, " Iliad not tasted driLk kr 17 years." Gradually slip- ping from fad to worse, he found in January last sear that he c'ould scarcely get downstairs. In me following month lie was admitted into the Military Hospital again, and transferad to Preston. On March 31st, 1893, he was discharged from the Service, on the recommend. don of the Army doctors, as. incurable. If ever there was a hopeless case, this *IA One. The doctors told him trar.kly that he could not live a twelve' month. yet, the twelvemonth just over, the reporter found Colgan, not merely alive, but active and well; he is, in fact, at work at the present day. The means of this remarkable escape remain to be told, and in reply to the reporter's inquiries it was elicited in simple and con- vincing terms. In his misery anti despair Colgan, help- less and useless, was beginning to feel that it would be best of all concerned if lie could di•J at once. Then, one day, his little son brought home a book in which were described some of the remarkable cures effected by the new medical discovery of Dr. Williams, an E duburgil University graduate—the remedy popularly known as Dr. Williams Pink • Pills for Pale Peopie. fhe patient scouted the idea of trying the pills. He was not going to wasteliis money—not he. Would he have been dis- charged from the Army, after the stringent examinatiou iequired in such cases, if he had' not been really incur- able 2.

He was talked into trying the remedy, however ; and from the first box the cure began. His pppe lite re- tumed,control of his limbs—of which the terrible disease locomotor ataxy had deprived hitn—returned also. T.. e extremities, formerly cold and limp, wero now tingling with new blood, and when the reporter saw Mr. Colaa he was walking every day to business—a man as surel snatched from death as though he had been caught upon the brink of the precipice.

That this is no vulgar mares'-nest , is shown by the investigation .conducted by a great newspaper, the " Preston Herald," and by the fact, also, that one of the very Army doctors who had pronounced the man's con- demuation had since seen him and admitted his cure, '

It is not alone in cases like the abaVe that Dr. Will'aftls' Pink Pill have a miraculous efficacy. They are also a perfect blood builder and nerve restorer, and an unfailing cure. for rheumatism, sciatica, neuralgia, partial paralysis, S. Vitus thrice, nervous headache, prostration, Scc. Dr. Williams' Pink Pills (which are not purgative, but strengthening) restore pale and sallow complexions to the glow of health, and are a specific for all the troubles peculiar to the female sex, while in the c.ises of mcn, they effect a ralic4l cure in all cases arising from mental worry, overwork,-or excesses of whatever nature.

file Pills are sold by all chemists, or may bis had direct from the Dr. Williams' Medicine Company, of 48, 1191 horn Viaduct, London, at 2s. 9d. a box, or six boxes for 134. 9,1. Never sold loose ; substitutes so sold are fraudulent. The wooden box must be in a pink wrap- per bearing the full name, Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People.

DREADFUL AFFAIR AT HIJYTON.

A MAN'S LEG cur OFF BY HOT SHEARS. Arthur Woo!tall, ironworker, employed at Huyton Quarry Ironworks, was brought before the magistrates on Tuesday, charged with doing grievous bodily harm to-a fellow woe er, Michael Hardman.—Superintendent Baxendale said that on Monday night, about a quarter- past eight, frr some unknown reason, the accused, it was alleged. struck Hardman, knocking him on to the machinery in motion, the hot shears cutting his leg off. He would offer some evidence shortly, and ask for a remand, sas the injured man was in a serious condition at the Liverpool Northern Hospital.—Jehn Beasley, electrician, of Liverpool-road,Tarbuck, employed at the works, said that both Woolfall and Hardman wore working at the rolling mills, and he saw the first-named strike Hardman, but from where he was he could not sea where he fell, bet, hearing a cry, he went round the mill, and saw him with his left leg nearly cut off by the hot shears that are constantly in motion. He bound the limb, and afterwards saw him to the Northern Hospital. —A remand was granted for eight days.

FON, r LIE PEOPLE.

THE BANDS ASSOCIATION.

By LIME BLUNT.

" Well, if I coesent to undergo the operation of heing interviewed you must promise me one thing?' "Oh yes! 111 promise anything." " Very possibly. But I want you to promise— And-to keep to it—that you do not go into gentle. cstncieA about my musical abilities:.

" Very well. But why not ? " " Because, though I am the secretary of the War- eingion Binds Association, I am Nnly a very .‘ howdy-do." sort of musician. As the saying goes I can just "daddy along " on an instrument; and 1 •lon't wish you to introduce any mild exaggeration upon that point." _ _

I respect your modesty Mr. Taylor. But, to get rØ business. suppose you tell me bow the Associv ion came to bo formed."

"Very well. It was the outcome of an agitation among the younger bands of the town, who wished to take their turn at playing in the Park. At that time there were only about four bands who gave public performances. Most of the other bands were in a rat her disorganized state. They generally played at Walking Day, and weren't heard again until Christmas when they came noising about town. When the younger bands brought their agi- tation before the Park Committee, it Wilb) explained that a new bandstand would be required. Several of the councillors refused to consi,;er this until they had proof that there would be properly or- ganized performances. Si) I was waited upon by a number of the bandsmen, and the result was the organization of the Association,which, as you stated last we.ek, includes 209 instrumentalists. The new bandstand was then erected. The old one, as a matter of fact, besides being too small, was unsafe. When the.men were Rprforming on it they were in a continual sweat lest the floor should give way.';

‘i By whom were the performances arranged be- fore the Association was form,d ?" "By five gentlemen who deservel the greatest of praise for their work :--Nlessrs. F. Monks, C.C., F.. W. Monks (junior), William Cleave, Shepherd, and Roorke. Those gentlemen worked most inde- fatigably for the institution. They used to go to an immense amount of trouble_ in collecting funds to pay the hands, some of whick came from Earies- town and Lymm and other districts."

" But is that not a generous remuneration, Mr. Taylor, considering that many people object to he bands being paid at all for ptitying on Sundays.?" • " Objections raised by ;zent lemen, some of whom earn their remuneration by preaching sermons on Sundays ! " I believe I know sufficient of these matters to confirm your assertion:t."

"This fact. I am certain of: We are doing a great deal of good in many ways. Numbers of labouring men, fOrgemen par- ticularly, have told me that the inu•qt in the park La a bright and pleasant change in what they find to he otherwise an uncheerful Sabbath. They. tell me that. formerly they had no desire to do anything except eat and drink and sleep. And it is for the pleasure of these people that, we wish to work particularly. When igo round with the col- lection box to these men, they invariably subscribe more than the orthodox penny. • But it, is not from them, abov,, anyone, that we ask tnore than "a copper."
