MORE THAN FIFTY YEARS A POSTMAN. Sir,-1 desire with 3 our generous aid to call the atten- tion 3f my fellow townsmen to the continuous and faithful service from 1843 to 1894 of James Pilling who has just retired from the service of the Post Office. Re bas served three successive generations, and preserved an irreproachable character. There are man 3 who think that the township (for many years he was the only post- man) should do something to mark its sense of such long service. The movement has the sympaths- and will have the practical support of Mr. McCorquodale, and I will gladly co-operate with any of my neighbours to promote it. " Fent qui meruit palmam."—Yours, Scc. JOHN W. WATKINS. Newton-le-Willows, 19 April, 1894.

WARRINGTON TOWN CLOCK. Sir,—Having reasen to believe that I quite misunder- stood the drift of Mr. Peer's remarks about the pendulum Suspension of the town clock, I shall be obliged if you will kindly allow me space for a few words of explana- tion. Mr. Peers expressed his opinions with such con- fident assurance that I really supposed that he had seen the clock, and knowing, as I do from personal inspec• tion, that the pendulum is suspended from the iron frame of the clock, and that there is nothing else near it which could by any stretch of imagination be regarded as a " gim-crack iron box," I naturally concluded that be referred to the clock trame. It now seems to me more probable that he was referring to that inelegant iron structure which contains the dials and dial wheels. Let me quote his own words. Speaking of Lord Grin- thorpe's book he says : " Nowhere do I find it recom- mending a shaky suspension in place of solid masonary. Every clock—turret or otherwise—should have the firmest possible suspension. No mere bolted iron work can in this respect compete with the mass of good solid masonry." If these words do not imply a belief that the town clock is not supported by solid masonry, but by bolted iron work, I don't know what they can mean. Taken in connection with his former remark about the pendulum being "suspended from a gins-crack iron box swaying by every breeze of wind," they seem only in- telligible on the supposition that Mr. Peers believes the clock te be fixed in the iron pepper box which caps the tower of Trinity Church. This is not so ; this structure contains enlv the dial work. The clock movement and pendulum are fixed some 201 t. or more lower down, below the belfry, and are supported by perfectly solid masonry. Whichever may be the correct interpretation of Mr. Peers's words his assertion about the pendulum suspension was unfortunate. If he knew whereabouts in the tower the clock is situated, and that it is support- ed by the masonry of the walls, his statement was sing- ularly ill-judged and misleading, and might with equal reason (or the reverse) he applied to probably ninety- nine out of every hundred turret clocks in the united kingdom made in recent times. If on the other hand he ' supposed the clock to be in the dial room at the top of the tower his knowledge of turret clocks 'rust indeed be deficient. It is by no means uncommon for such clocks to be fixed at a considerably lower level than their dials and no one, aware of this fact, would suppose the clock in Trinity tower to be fixed in such an unsuitable struc- ture as the dial room, even if there were room enough. I will leave your readers to infer from Mr. Peers's own letters which of the two alternatives I have suggested is the more probable. In either case his assertion that the pendulum suspension is "bad" is unaue. In no respect does it differ from that of other clocks which I have seen by Gillet, Joyce, Potts, or Smith, all makers of repute. Under the circumstances it would be superfluous to consult Lord Griinthorpe, which would, hewever, be no new experience to me as Mr. Peers assumes, jumping to conclusions again, for I have on several occasions sought advice from his Lordship en horological matters and always received very courteous replies. There are two or three features in the construction of the town clock which I know that Lord Grimthorpe would disapprove of, but they are not of such a nature as to prevent it , keeping good time, and he would assuredly say, if he saw the clock, that there is no reason why it should not go to a few seconds a week. That it has done so fre- quently I have ample evidence in the records which I have kept for the past seven years. Referring to those I find its variations have been much oftener under than over 5 seconds a week, neglecting ing the variatien (2i secs.) due to want of precision in the • strike. Unforturately I have shenn myself so "incapable or neglectful " and " so conspicuously lack- ing" in accuracy of method that I cannot of course expect this statement to he accepted as true. lam moreover forgetting that I have little or no evidence to prove that the clock may not have been seriously wrong between the dates of observation,beyond the fact that it used to go uniformly from day to day with another clock in my own possession. lam afraid this won't no. The two clocks perhaps always happened to vary at the same time, and in the same direction ; SO I must give it up. Speaking seriously I don't expect everybody to ac- cent my statements without reserve ; but I may at least ask these who are incredulous as to the accuracy of the clock, to suspend their jedgement until it has been proved unreliable. This must be my last word on the subject.—Yours truly,

F. W. REYNOLDS.

THE DEATH OF HERR PRAEGER.

A TRIBUTE FROM MR. T. J. DOWN. At the Warrington Cemetery on Monday there were interred amid many tokens of respect, the re- mains of the late Ilerr Praeger, whose death was recorded last week. The funeral cortege left the late residence of the deceased in Lovely-lane shortly after two a'clock,the chief mourners being—Mr.O.l. Praeger, Mr. F. W Praeger, Mr. L. A. Praeger, Mr. A.B. Praeger,and Mr. J.P. Praeger (sons), Mr. Ralph Johnson (son-in-law), Mr. Winter, Preston(brot her- in-law), Mr. Hopton, Preston (brother-in-law), Mr. Greenwood, Preston ; Mr.G. J. Allen, and Mr.Harter. Among those invited were Messrs. John Hs.mpson, Dockrey, R. R. Forster, J. G. Allen, John Smith A. Down, G. Johnstone, C. Lawton,R.Whitfield,Thomas Mather,Peter Mather,Robert Wilson, John Caldwell, Percy Randall,R. 11. Pearson,and W.H. Payton. The pall bearers were Messrs. W. Charnock, jun.,P.Cald- Walsh,Whitehead, riughes,and J.Burrows,who were formerly pupils of the deceased. The coffin, which was of fumigated oak,with brass mountings, bore the inscription—"lsouard Oreste Praeger, died April 12th, 1804, aged 63 years?' The Rev. C. E. Carleton was the officiating clergyman. A number of wrEaths were received from the following : Mrs. Praeger (the widow), Mr.and Mrs.llalph John- son, Mr. and Mrs. Potter, Mr. and Mrs. J. H. Cald- well, Mr. and Mrs. °leave, Mr. G. J. Allen. Mr. and Mrs. Wakefield. Mrs. Brierley, Mr. and Mrs.liopton, Mr. and Mrs. Winter, Mr. and Mrs. Greenwood, Mr. Widow, and Mr. and Mrs. R H. Pearson. During Saturday over a hundred telegrams of condolence were received from the Continent from deceased's relatives and friends. - - - -

On the assembling of his orchestra fer the weekly practice on Wednesday Mr. Down alluding to the recent death of a local musician said :—What Mr. Praeger's death means to many of us in this orches- tra is not easily expressed in w.ards. I feel sure that all of us who had the privilege of knowing him deeply deplore his loss. Mr. Praeger was no ordinary man. He was undoubtedly a genius. He was certainly eccentric as men of more than ordin- ary talent are, but he was a true-hearted man, and thoroughly loyal to his friends. He was an excel- lent musician, and had the power of gaining the at- tention and affection of his pupils such as is pos- sessed by few teachers. I consider that he has done more to further the cause of music in Warrington than any other man. He did not however confine his studies to music. He was deeply read in mv.ny other subjects, and there was nothing I enjoyed more than to be in his society, and to listen to his interesting and instructive conversation. lam oc- casionally complimented upon what I have done for the cause of music, and am told that to have established and carried on so long the two _Bands which meet in this room is a wonderful work. Well, perhaps it is, but common honesty compels me to admit that a very large share of the credit of it is due to our deptrted friend. I feel certain that , if Mr. Praeger had not come to Warrington these bands would never have come into existence at all. When this Orchestra was started it consisted al- most entirely of Mr. Praeger's pupils and without them could not have been carried on, and to this day they are the back-bone of the band. The Mili- tary Band was suggested and got together by the wind players in the Orchestra, but it was Mr. Praeger who made the Orchestra possible, and from this the other sprang by a sort of natural develop. ment. lat any rate can never forget our old friend nor how much, as a musician I am indebted to him. As a token of their united respect for his memory the orchestra then performed the " Dead March " in .Saul.

IMPENDING STOPPAGE IN TRE ALKALI TRADE. Trade and business generally in the St. Helens dis- trict shows very little sign of improvement since the settlement of the coal strike, and complaints are heard on all sides. The foreign competition in the glass trade is so keen that it is stated one firm is losing large si,mi weekly. On the Ist of May a general strike is threatened in the building trade, the men demanding an advance and the masters insisting on a reduction. In addition now comes the report that a general stoppoge is to take 'place in the chemical trade. Although no official state- ment has yet been made, it is understood that at Whit- suntide, work at the whole of the establishments in St. Helens and Widnes owned b 7 the Alkali UM -m will be completely suspended for a fortnight or three weeks,and thousands of men will be thrown out of employment. It is understood that the Alkali Union is completely gutted with stocks, and the cessation of work is necces- , sary to reduce them.

RUNCORN.

===== 0002601_18940421_art0156.txt | included=true | included =====
BY A.N OCCASIONAL CORRESPONDENT.

We have been favoured with a great amount of our Parliamentary member's attention during his brief visit on Saturday. Not only did we see him performing the ceremoli: of laying the foundation stone of the new Institute of Technical Instruction, and beard him express his entire concurrence with the movement for the advancement of learning, but we saw him the same afternoon extending a help- ing hand to a local organization in a rather novel form. Perhaps we might say extending a helping foot, because the action referred to was his kicking off at the football match between Runcorn and Sal- ford which ended in the homesters winning such a splendid victory over their powerful opponents. The club's exchequer must be materially improved, as the "gate" was a record one.

Thus, in one afternoon, we find Mr. Brunner showing his sympathy with both physical and men- tal exercises, and to the latter, it might be said, in a very substantial manner. Towards the building of the Institute he had already promised £5OO, but on Saturday afternoon he displayed another ex- ample of his generosity by promising a further £5OO providing Runcorn subscribed an additional sum to that amount. Here is an opportunity for the people of R.incorn to follow the example of their member, and everyone will admit that it is a worthy example. It is to be hoped that, notwith- standing the present depressed state of trade, those who can afford will give, in order that the wish of the majority of the people may be gratified, namely, that the Institute shall be opened free from debt.

Tie Liberals who were assembled in the Liberal Club on Saturday evening must have felt highly delighted when Mr. Brunner made his appearance amongst them once more, and those members who were not present certainly missed a rare treat. As upon former oclasions Mr. Brunner delivered an address in his customary style, first dealing with the retirement of Mr. Gladstone from the premier- ship, then with the form which he expected the budget to take, and finally with the recent stupid and crazy attempt of the Tory party to legislate. He congratulated the members upon the comfort- able and jolly appearance of the room, and hoped when they had succeeded in paying off the debt, that they would not "blackball "the young Liberals who came after them,but extend a hearty welcome. The proceedings were characterized throughout by genial enthusiasm and confidence in Mr. Brunner as their Parliamentary representative.

Still another evidence of the deep interest which Mr. Brunner manifests in the welfare of Runcorn. It is known that some time ago he purchased the old Presbyterian chapel and schools with a view to their being converted into a United Trades Hall. The matter was then put into the hands of delegates from the local trades unions representing nearly a thousand members, and these gentlemen decided that the better course would be to pull the old building down entirely and erect new premises. The total cost of the scheme was estimated at £7OO, and plans were prepared of the proposed building. On Saturday evening Mr. Brunner had an interview with the delegates and approved of the plans like- wise generously offering to detray the whole cost and hand the building over to some public trust. Need- less to mention the offer was gratefully accepted and Mr. Brunner received the thanks of the dele- gates for his munificence.

THE AUTHORITY AND MR SMALLPDX HOSPITAL.

The Ctunic stated that in accordance with the Authority's instructions to make the best terms he could with Mr. Jackson, in conjunction with Dr. Adams, for the purchase of the M.S.C. hospital, he had consulted with Dr: Adams. THE AUTHORITY AND THE IkIANCHESTER SHIP CANAL DILL.

Mr. Brandreth presented the report of the Committee appointed to secure the insertion of the clause in the above bill referring to the Authority's drains and sewers likely to be affected by the undertaking. The result of the Committee's deliberations was that the Authority should employ Parliamentary agents to watch the Bill as it passed through Parliament giving to the Committee full powers to act in case of emergency. Also that the Clerk press the solicitors of the Company to send a draft of the clause and whilst doing so inform them that in con- sequence of not having sent it the Authority had been forced to instruct Parliamentary agents to watch the progress of the,Bill, any expenses entailed by this action, ' through the solicitors delay, to be paid by the Company. Mr. Brandreth concluded by proposing the adoption of the report.—Mr. Heywood seconded and the repJrt was adopted.

rINANCIS COMMITTEE.

FATAL RAILWAY A-CCIDENT.—An accident of a shock- ing nature occurred shortly before seven o'clock on Thursday morning on the London and North-Western Railway at Helsby. A man named Joseph Lightfoot,un- married, and a native of the village, by occupation a plate-layer, was attempting to evade a passenger train, and whilst doing so was knocked down and run over by a goods train travelling between Chester and Warring- ton. Both legs were severed from his body. Dr. Bry- ant, of Helsby, was at once communicated with, but notwithstanding his skill and attention the poor fellow succumbed to his injuries at 8.20 the same morning. The circumstances have been reported to the Coroner, who will probably investigate the Mintter.

THE SHIP CANAL.

THE FINANCIAL POSITION OF THE COMPANY.

The following statement was issued on Monday, by the Board of Directors :—From the statement sub- mitted to the Ship Canal Bard on Saturday the 14th Inst. by the Chairman of the Finance Committee, Sir Edward Jenkinson, it appears that the cash balances and other available assets, including the half million remaining out of the two millions authorised to be lent by the Manchester Corporation under the Act of 1893, amounted on the Ist January, 1894, to £1,353,482. The following is the forecast of the financial position of the Company on the Ist January, 1895 :—After pro-. viding for all works already ordered by the Executive Committee (including six dock sheds three storeys hih and twenty hydraulic cranes ordered since the date of the shareholders meeting in February last), for dredging, for the balance owing on purchases of land, for working expenses, and for interest On all loans for the year 1894, and taking traffic receipts at a low estimate, the sales of plant at only £20,000 (£12,000 having been realised since January 1), and without estimating any receipts from sales of land, there is a balance of £331,515. _ _

Some portion of this balance will probably bit ex- pended by the Executive Committee on further neces- sary works. The Company owns abtut 2,600 acres of surplus lands, the value of which is already large and will steadily increase. The value of the plant now avail- able for sale is also very considerable. IMPERIAL PARLIAMENT.

THURSDAY. In the House .of Comirons on Thursday evening Mr. H. GARDNER, answering a question about the importa- tion of foreign meat, said he hoped to be able to intro- duce at an early date a bill giving the Board of Agricul- ture, in cases affecting the general interests of agriculture powers similar to those conferred on the Board of Trade by the Merchandise Marks Act. Questioned about the extradition of Mr. J. S. BAL- vont, Sir E. GREY said Her Majesty's Government had takm every legal precaution necessary. No fresh ob- stacles had arisen, but it was not yet possible to say when the legal proceedings would come to an end. Sir W. HARCOURT, in reply to questions, said he hoped to be able to find an opportunity nest week for the Welsh Disestablishment Bill. In answer to a question put by Mr. SEXTON, the right hon gentleman said that, having regard to the Rending inquiry into the relative incidence of taxation in the separate parts of the United Kingdom, he thought it was a fair demand and was right that the proposals for an increase in the duty on spirits and beer should take effect only for the present financial year.

Mr. SITAW-LEFETRE brought in a bill to accelerate the registration of parochial electors in the present year. He said it had been arranged that the measure should be treated as non-contentious, and it would be possible to hold the first meetings of the parish councils within a few days after the 22nd November. Mr. Mott-ism introduced the Government bill to facili- tate and make provision for the restoration of Irish evicted tenants to their holdings. In the course of his statement he said the foundation of their prop )sals was the constitution of a board of three arbitrators, whose office was to last for two years. The tenant would be reinstated subject to certain conditions. He would go back provisionally at the old rent, but this would be transformed into a fair rent after investigation. The arbitrators might direct payment to the landlt rd, for arrears of rent and costs, for a sum n.)t exceeding two years of the old rent, and they might also make to the landlord a free grant of one-half of that sum from money placed at their disposal by Parliament, the other half being secure.d or paid by the tenant. The landlord might require the tenant to purchase instead el taking the land as a tenant. As t) funds, it was proposed that £lOO,OOO should be chargeable on the Irish Church Temporalities Fund. Mr. Balfour briefly criticised the bill. Mr. Harrington and Mr. T. W. Russell expressed disappointment with the bill. Mr. Dir,Lox thought the bill contained valuable provisions and would put four- fifths of the evicted tenants back in their homes. Mr. COURTNEY said whether this was a practical attempt or not, they could but hope in its practicability. Mr. W. REDMOND said the difficulty of the 66 planters" would not be settled. Mr. SEXTON said the bill was as good a bill as they were likely to pass, and with much gratitude he should vote for the second reading. Mr. MORLEY expressed himself gratified with the reception accorded to the bill, which was read a first time. Mr. Muodell a afterwards moved the second reading of the Co nciliation Bill, but it was talked out by Mr. Pierpoint.

===== 0002601_18940421_art0168.txt | included=true | included =====
--s- PENKETH.

FAREWELL MEETING. —Last evenieg week, the em- ployees of the Penketh Tanning, Co. assembled at the Wesleyan School with their families to partake of the tea provided for them by Mr. Charles Parker, the mana- ger of the tannery, who has left for America. The tea was very much enjoyed by all and after the close the room was cleared, and a concert was held, the room being densely crowded by the tanners with their wives and families. Mr. James Dodds, of Lymtn, was in the chair and called on Mr. Welch to commence the pro- gramme with "Tom Bowling." This was followed by 1 Mr. Norman who wq,s loudly applauded for "My Dear Old Wife." Miss E. Tetlow sang a song, and which was followed by a reading by G. Smith. A duet by Messrs. Appleton and Budden, followed by a song from F. Goul- den brought the first part to a close. Mr. Dodds ad- dressed the meeting, and spoke of the occasion that had brought them together that evening. They were there to bid God speed to a man who was respected and ad- mired by all. His conduct towards the workmen under him had been honourable and straightforward and he had promoted various set-Imes which were to bring benefit to the village. He hoped Mr. Parker would have a pleasant trip and return with renewed health and ex- perience. Mr. Parker, who was received with uproari- ous applause, then addressed the meeting. He felt quite touched by their expressions of esteem and good wishes, but he did not think Mr. Dodds was quite correct in say- ing he had done all the work in the matter of improve- ments. The workmen he thought were very industrious. and he was quite satisfied with them. The trade at the tannery however was falling off considerably, and the last year was the worst that had been experienced by the firm. The leather manufacturers of America could supply the same quality of leather cheaper than they could and were spoiling their trade. He had decided to take a trip to America to see if he could find the secret of their trade, and when he re:urned hoped to be able to improve their manufacture and lessen their expenditure. He felt that he was leaving men behind him that he could trust, and felt sure all would go well in his absence. The memory of their meeting would be a pleasant recol- lection for him when away. Messrs. Norman and Pos- nett also addressed the meeting. A second part of the programme was then gone through mid the meeting closed with the singing of "He's a jelly good fellow." Mr. Parker left the village on Saturday to board the American boat at Liverpool. He expects to be away about two months.

THE COAL TRADE.

DEPRESSION AND LABOUR DIFFICULTIES.

A Chesterfield telegram says the coal trade outlook in Derbyshire is extremely gloomy. At Kilburn Colliery the men are on strike against the proposal of the mas- ters, which it is said will affect the men to the exbent of is. 6d. or 23. per, day. The Miners' Union supports the men's action. The Manvers Colliery Company are disposing of the services of 120 men on the ground that their furnace coal seam is not being worked at a profit. It is estimated that there are several thou sand Derby- shire miners idle, and that many more wcrk very short time.

THE S.S. "GOTHIC."

The White Star Mail Steamer "Gothic," which in December last, on her arrival in the Thames from the building yard of Messrs. Harland and Wolff, Belfast,was visited by H.R.H. The Duchess of Albany, and after- wards thrown open to the public, for the benefit of the " Dreadnaught " Seamen's Hospital, whereby over £4OO was collected on behalf of the charity, arrived back in London, from New Zealand, early on Sunday morning last, after the completion of a most successful maiden voyage. Amongst her passengers was Sir Geore Grey. Throughout the round voyage the vessel maintair.ed an exceptionally high rate of speed, and she arrived in the Royal Albert Dock, on her return trip, three days ahead of her itinerary, after one of the quickest and most enjoyable passages yet made from New Zealand. It is interesting to note that the "Gothic" is the first steamer in the New Zealand service fitted with " Carbonic Anhydride System of Refrigeration." which has worked so successfully in the Atlantic and River Plate Dead Meat Trades, and it augurs well for the future of this method that 70,000 carcases of mutton and 2,400 packages of butter arrived by the " Gothic " in perfect condition.

HOUSEBREAKING AT NEWTON.

EXTENSIVE THEFT.

At Newton Petty Sessions on Saturday, Joseph Booth, about sixty years of age, was charged with having on the 7th April broken and entered into the house of Richard Coppul, and stolen therefrom two silver watches and one gold chain value £l4. Richard Coppul stated that he was a thrashing ma- chine owner and lived at 430, Wargrave-road, New- ton-le-Willows. He had known the prisoner for six months and he had employed him at the thrashing machine. In company with his wife he (witness) left home on the 7th inst, and before leaving he securely fastened the windows and doors. After leaving the house he met the prisoner in Red Bank-lane, Newton, and told him to go to the machine which was then at the Hermit Inn, and to do some repairs to the sheet which covered it, and which had a rent in it. He gave the prisoner sixpence, and told him he was going to Croft. He and his wife returned home at 11.30 the same day and found the back window broken and the catch pushed back. He went upstairs and found that the dressing-table drawers had been ransacked, and the drawer which contained the jewellery on the floor. His watch and his wife's watch and chain were missing,and he valued them at £l4-. He identified the watches and chain produced as his property.—Mrs. Hannah Coppul, wife of the previous witness corroborated her husband's statement.—Edgar Stockwell, assistant to Mr. Thomas Greaves, pawnbroker, Church-street, Warrington, said at nine o'clock on the 9th inst., the prisoner came to the shop at Warrington and offered for pledge the gent's watch produced. The prisoner gave the name of John , Taylor, Back Crossley-street, Warringten, and said the I watch belonged to hiai, and had been given to him by his brother-in-law, Richard Ceppul.—He advanced him 10s. on it. P.C. Schofield stated that on the Bth inst. he apprehended the prisoner at Forshaw's lodging-house, Earlestown. Witness asked him if he had anything in his pockets which did not belong to him. Prisoner replied "no." On searching him he (witness) found upon him the ladies' watch and chain prcduced, and also a broken watch glass. Be took him to the police station and charged him with the offence as set forth on the charge sheet. The prisoner said "I didn't do it." He (witness) received the gent's watch produced from Mr.Greavee pawnshop, Warring- ton. The prisoner who has been several times convicted at Warrington was committed to Liverpool Quuter Sos- sioas.

THE NEW TECHNICAL INSTITUTE FOR

RUNCO R N. MR. J. T. BRUNNER, M.P. LAYS THE FOUNDA-

TION STONE.

The important ceremony of laying the founda- tionstone of the new Technical Institute, at Run- corn, was performed on Saturday last by Mr. J. T. Brunner, M.P., for this division, in the presence of several thousand people. The centre of operations in Waterloo-road, was hung with a profusion of bunting, whilst above the Town Hall, and at differ- ent parts of the site, flags were hoisted. The Sea- men's Institute also came in fer similar decorations, which together with the dressings of various other buildings in the town had the effect of making the scene quite gay and festive. Shortly after one o'clock large crowds of people came streaming into the town from tha railway station and many other directions, all mak- ing their way, more or less, towards the Town Hall, in Wa,erloo-roasi. Three brass bands had kindly lent their services, namely, Volunteer Band, Weston Wesleyan,and the Weston Point Saltworks, all of whom, at intervals, discoursed various selections of music. At about two o'clock Mr. Brunner arrived at the Town Hall accom- panied by some of the Commissiomers and officials, and was greeted with a hearty welcome upon his entrance into the Boardroom,where the remaining Commissioners and other gentlemen were assembled. The procession was then formed and proceeded, in company with the bands named, towards the site of the Institute. Mr. S. Taylor, as chairman of the Commissioaers,presided over the proceedings, amongst those gentlemen present being the following: Tile Rev. Canon Maitland Weed, J. Hei- den, J. Simpson, W. Arrowsmith,and T. Casely ; Messrs. P. Speakman, J.P., C.C., J. Mealowcroft, H. J. Grice, W. Hankinson, T. Lea, J. W. Lightburn, F. Wiswall,E. Imison, D. Bisbrown, R. Abel, J. Pritchard, and W. Elandley,(Commissioners), J. Bowden, (architect), J. R. Frith, J. Moss, J. D. Jones, T. Baxter, C.C., T. Hanley, F. J. Norman, J.P., W. Wright, C.C., (Seacombe), A.R. Norman, J. B. Holland, R. Rawlinson, F. A. Lake, M. Barker, E. Marshall, J. Wilding, W. E. Dudlev,T. Riley R. Boyle, W. Rhodes, W. Cropper, T. Bowyer,T. Taylor J. Kirkham, J. Billing,ton, J. Holt, William Moston, E. Aston, Clare, W. E. Shore, L. Rozenbury, W. Shaw, J. Percival, R. Wright, J. Bellamy, S. R tvensc nit, J. W. Woodland, J. F. Moulton, C. Pownall, G. Dutton, V. Jones, J. Gatley, B. Crosby, Captain J. W. Boyle, W. Joynson, T. Boote, T. Cooke, Jabuz Stubbs, &c. See. Mr. Taylor, who was loudly applauded, expressed his great pleasure at seeing such a large number of persons present. He was convinced, judging from the enthusiasm generally displayed that they had the sympathy of the people of Runcorn in this important and very beneficial undertaking. (Applause.) Tiiey all knew that the future benefit of this country entirely depended upon education. Other nations were against us, and they had made great strides for veers past in matters concerning education. He was how- ever, very pleased to find that England was, and had been for a long time, acting a great part in this ques- tion. (Hear, hear.) They ell knew Inivr much more pleasant and beneficial it was for both employers and employed, when the latter were educated men, and consequently understood the nature of the trades at which they worked. An educated man always listened to reason and if he found that his employer was in the right, he would no longer contend against him, but with an uneducated man it was sometimes different. They had made great ttrides in both ele- mentary education, and technical instruction, and he thought, that everyone would agree with him that in the near future they would have advantages for all classes. In Runcorn, he was very pleased to say, they had kapt abreast in educational matters of any town the same size. (Applaese.) Mr. Brunner was a thorough educationalist and had done a great deal in forwarding the cause. He had built a very handsome free library for Northwich, as well as assisting Wins- ford, Mickliewich, and other towns in Cheshire. (Ap- plause.) Under those circumstances he was sure he • would be tight in saying that Mr. Brunner's heart and soul were in education, and he had come there that day te lay the foundation stone, they would all be very proud to see him. (Loud applause.) Mr. Wiswall, chairman of the Technical Instruction Committee, then introduced Mr. John Bowden, the architect of the Institute, and spoke at seine length on that gentleman's experience and capability. He was sure Mr. Bowden had done the work to the very best advantage, and he had great pleasure in calling upon him to assist Mr. Brunner in laying the foundaticn stone. _

Mr. Bowden, having presented the silver trowel and mallet to Mr. Brunner, delivered a short address, after which the formal ceremony of laying the stone was gone through. When it waes concluded, Mr. Brunner said : I declare this stone to be well and truly laid.--(Loud applause.)—ln the cavity beneath the stone a bottle was placed containing a number of small coins, a copy of the Examiner and other local papers, and the Commissioners' year book. Canon Wood, in moving a vote of thanks to Mr. Brunner,said that many people in Runcorn would like a public park and play ground, and others were seeking to secure public baths. They had, however, something that day of an extremely progressive nature. (Applause.) He then proceeded to deal at some length with the benefits which would accrue from the teaching at such an Institute as the one in course of erection. The present occasion was one upon which Conservatives and Liberals could rear together and unitedly thank their member,Mr. Brunner, for laying the foundation stone. (Loud cheers.) Mr. Speakman expressed the great pleasure he felt in seconding the resolution of thanks to Mr. Brunner. He was glad to say that in conjunction with himself the Improvement Commissioners had taken up the question of building the Institute in so noble a manner that the result was that great progress had been made. Mr. Brunner had set a noble example by giving £5OO. and in laying the foundation stone of the building in which the minds of so many young people would be developed. (Applause.) Mr. Pritchard, chairman of the School Board, sup- ported the motion. For the last three years the Science and Art classes had been concructed by the School Board with considerable success, and lie hoped that the same success would attend the Institute when it was com- pleted. Mr. Brunner was an ardent educationalist and was a generous elver to institutions which advanced the social and moral condition of the people. He had great pleasure in supporting the resolution which he was sure would be heartily approved.. Mr. W. Wright (Liscard) also supported the motion with great pleasure, and added that lie was pleased to see how Runcorn was advancing in matters concerning, the education of its people. Mr. Taylor then put the resolution which was carried with acclamation and waving of hats. Mr. Brunner in acknowledgement of the vote, said it was with great pleusure that he found himself in Run- corn that day engaeed in so good a work as that placed in his hands by the governing authority of the town. (Hear, hear.) There was no subject to which they could more worthily give their time, attention, money and work, than the subject of education. (Hear, bear, and applause.) The people in England had been very sadly behind in educational matters, and it was the greatest pleasure to him that day to see an indication of the intention of the people of Runcorn to go forward in the work. lie hisped they would soon make up their minds to do the best they possibly could to overtake the ' French, Germans, and Swiss, and then not to be con- tent until they had beaten them hapds down. There was nothing 'which Englishmen could not do better than any other nation if they only made up their minds to ' do it, and he hoped they would make up their minds to do this. (Applause.) he cordially concurred with the statement recently made by Canon Wood that they were all of one mind that day, and that no political difficulties divided them. When the beautiful building was completed, and those attending the classes elevated to a moral atmostphere, they could then work with unanimity and he happy. He asked them all to loek forward to meeting in the building once a year to see ' the work of the scholars, and the presentatirn of the re- wards, and expressed a hope that the fathers and mothers of the unsuccessful scholars would take an equal interest in the event with the fathers and mothers of the successful scholars, and admire with unselfishness the rewards given to the latter. (Applause.) Runcorn had done well in adopting the Free Libraries Act, and the Technical Instruction Act which had exercised a most beneficial effect upon the town. Owing to the two Acts named the maximum rate was one penny in each case, but the School Board might gather up as large an amount of money froes the pockets of the ratepayers as they were willing to give. He was looking forward to the day when the debt upon the schools held by the trustees of the School Board, would he paid off. They knew that the School Board rate included not

--- only the annual expenses of carrying on the institution, but also instalments for the repayment of borrowed

- _ capital. Some day that debt would be wiped off and he was going to boast on their behalf that when that had been accomplished, the ratepayers would not spend less, but more on the education of their children. It had been said that he ‘Mr. Brunner) would give £5OO if Runcorn would subscribe £5OO. The latter amount had not been subscribed, and consequently his money had not been paid. (Laughter.) He thought £5OO was a very small sum for a town like Runcorn to raise. In a large number of small towns in Wales as much as £1 per head had been subscribed for secondary schools. If the people of Runcorn would subscribe like that they would soon get £20,000, instead of £5OO. (Hear, hear and laughter.) Continuing, Mr. Brunner said that as he sympathised so much with the work they had in hand he was ready to promise a further £5OO, if Runcorn would do likewise. ,Loud cheers.) He felt rather ashamed of himself for not having mentioned the girls of Runcorn, and he would therefore like for all fathers and mothers to understand that ample facilitir s for the girls as well as the boys would be provided at the institute. He hoped they would get their County Council scholar- ships and thus be able to go to better schools than Run- corn could afford them. (Hear, hear.) He would close his remarks as he began them by saying t}..a`, it had been a great pleasure to him to leave a region of strife at Westminster and come to a region of contentment in Runcorn. (Laughter.) He hoped• that good times were in store for Runcorn, when, ttrough the work

executed by those whom they elected to represent them, aided by the ministers of religion in the town, that their population might be so raised in character, in capability, and conduct, that they might live to see the day when all brutality and vice would have vanished from the town, and that poverty, except in the cases of infirmity and advanced years, might be banished. (Loud cheers.)

Mr. F. J. Norman,J.P., in proposing a vote of thanks to the Commissioners and the Technical Instruction Committee, ceupled the names of Messrs. Taylor and Wiswall, the respective chairmen. He need hardly remind them that an immense amount of work had to be done in an undertaking of that kind, which was always beset with so many difficulties. It might be fairly argued that the resources of the gentlemen named were too limited, in a towu so small as Runcorn, for a scheme so ambitious. They must not make a mistake when he said this.. He did not complain that a section of the population should take that view, but he merely mentioned it to demonstrate the difficulty of their task. Fortunately the Committee had a magnificent faith which over-rode all obstacles, and by its deeds in the past, and by its promises for the future, it well earned their thanks. In his introduction to the four essays on political econerny, John Ruskin urged that there should be training schools established, at Government cost, over the whole country; that every child born in the country should imperatively be taught with the best skill the country could produce the following three things :—The laws of health and the exercises enjoined by them ; gentleness and justice ; the calling by which they are to live. That was a very high ideal of technical education, considered chimerical be the vast majority. But already it was even approaching realization; and the completion of that institute would enable Runcorn to comply with its exacting demands. As they had heard, the State would only furnish part of the cost,and although Mr. Brunner had nobly doubled his princely contribution ot £5OO there was still a large amount to be raised by private contributions. He suggested that if some wealthy gentleman could not be found to give £l,OOO, that the sum should be acqui red by gathering small subscriptions. (Applause.) The motion was seconded by Mr. Raw linson, sup- ported by Mr. John Holt, and heartily accorded. Mr. Taylor in responding said it was his great desire to see the building completed and free from debt. They had an income of £4OO, but they had to interfere with that sum fer the erection of the building. However, he hoped everyone would do their best to free the Institute before it was opened.

Mr Wigwall alter returning thanks said the building of the Institute .would cost £4,000. They were guaran- teed £3 for every £1 subscribed'so that hews certain if everyone tried their hardest the schools would be open free from debt.

The proceedings then terminated, after cheers having been raised for Mr. Brunner and his wife, which were briefly acknowledged. The foundation stone which is of white granite bore the following inscription ;—• "This foundatinn stone was ! laid by J. T. Brunner Esq., M.P., on April 14th, 1894." ' The trowel was incrihed with the following words : "Presented to J. T. Brunner Esq., M.P., on the occasion of laying the foundation stone of the Runcorn Technical Institute, April 14th, 1894." The amount promised arid subscribed is £2,500, which with the additional £5OO promised by Mr. Brunner, as mentioned above, will bring the total up to £3,000. The following donations, which have been included in the above total, have been ! promised :--The Rev. Canon Maitland Wood, £5O; Mr. P. Speakman, J.P., C.C., £5O; Mr. F. J. Norman, J.P., £5O; Mr. S. Taylor, J.P., £3O; Mr. W. Flankinson, ; Mr. H. J. Grice, £5; Mr. W. Handley, £5; Mr. F. Wis- wall, £5; Mr. R. Ravilinson, £5; Mr. Jno. Holt, £2. Messrs. F. Wiswall and Aloston have also promised to raise £5 each. These amount A together with £l,OOO granted by the Science and Art Department, £440 by the Cheshire County Council, and £3OO accumulated by the Commissioners from the penny rate bring the total !up to the figures mentioned above. The Technical In- struction Committee are making strenuous exertions to ! secure the necessary balance, and it is to be hoped tlast their endeavours will be rewarded with success.

Regarding the dispute at the Prescot Watch Factory, the man offered to return at a red uced rate, but their services were not required. 8) much inconvenience was caused by certain of the hands leaving work on the Grand National day, that the management de- cided to substitute machinery for labour, and this has been done.

AFFAIRS OF A ST. HELENS JOINER AND BUILDER. At the Liverpool Bankruptcy Court, on Thursday,John Fisher, a joiner and builder at Sutton, near St. Helens, came up for public examination. The liabilities were returned at -2158, and the assets at £2lB, the latter in- cluding a reversionary interest and an equity in some property.—The case was of an ordinary nature, and the examination was closed. SUDDEN DEATH IN THE MARKET. Some consternation was caused in St. Helens Market about eleven o'clock on Monday morning by the sudden death of an elderly man named Samuel Winstanley, be- lieved to be a resident in Gerard's Bridge. A bottle of medicine was found in his pocket, and it transpired that he had just come from the surgery of Dr. Wyse. The body was removed to the public mortuary.

ST. HELENS T. STOCKPORT.

At S. Helens, on Thursday evening, before 2,000 spectators. The game was fairly even in the first half, Chorlton and King scoring tries for Stockport, which Saville convert9d, and Sudlow gained an unimpnlved try for St. Helens. The home team played brillian'Ay in the second half, and tries were scored by Appleton, Berry and Sudlow (two), none of which were converted. St. Helens won by five tries (15 points) to two goals (10 points).

===== 0002601_18940421_art0175.txt | included=true | included =====
A LIBERAL CANDIDATE FOR Sr. BELEM.

Mr. William Woods, J.P., ex-Mayor of Wigan, has de- finitely declined the invitation of the St. Helens Liberal Association to contest St. Helens in opposition to Mr. Henry Seton-Karr at the next election. At a largely at- tended meeting of the Executive Committee of the St. Helens Liberal Association on Tuesday night, the ques- tion of a Liberal candidate for the borough was con- sideredi and it was unanimously decided to recommend the association to invite C.uncdlor John Forster, J.P., chairman of the Executive Committee of the association to contest the borough at the next election.

===== 0002601_18940421_art0176.txt | included=true | included =====
MAY DAY PROCESSION ABANDONED.

The Borough Surveyor (Mr. Broom) reported to the Horses Committee of the St. Miens Corporation on Wed- nesday that he had prepared a report on the question of a May Day demonstration. The harness decorations cost the C ,rporation about £2O, and the cost of extra horses, loss of work, and disorganisation generally amounted to between £BO and £lOO. He suggested that instead of a turnout they should give the men a supper. The Chairman said there had been a strong feeling ex- pressed that it was only right that the public should see a full turnout of the Corporation horses, and let them see they had as fine a set of horses as any in Lan- epshire,—The surveyor's suggestion of a supper was adopted.

===== 0002601_18940421_art0177.txt | included=true | included =====
PROPOSED PICTURE EZEIBITION.

At the monthly meeting of the Parks Committee of the St. Helens Corporation on Wednesday, Councillor Mar- tin presiding, Mr. A. Lancaster, curator of the Victoria Park Museum, reported that he had communicated with Messrs. Cassell and Co., Art publishers, of London, with a view to their lending for a given period during the summer a number of their original black and white drawings, engravings, &c., for a free exhibition at the Museum. Messrs. Cassell had replied that they would not be able to supply any original picturßs until about the third week in June. But at that time they wou!d be happy to send some pictures. The Chairman said the only expenses would be railway carriage.—On the notion of M. Urmson, seconded by Mr. Burchall, it was decided to accept the offer of Messrs. Cassell.

THE INFECTIOUS DISEASES ACT.

At St. Helens Police Court, on Saturday,before Coun- cillor J. B. Leach, Dr. Robertson, assistant medical officer of health, made an application, under section 10 of the Infectious Diseases Prevention Act, for an order authorising the removal of the dead body of a man to the mortuary. The body was that of George Marsh, who had died on Thursday night from typhoid fever at his house in Cook's-fold, Windle City. At the house there was one room downstairs and two upstairs. The body lay in the room on the ground floor where the in- mates were living, and where all the cooking was going on. The consequence was that there was a danger of the infections spreading. He asked for the body to be removed to the mortuary and buried that day.—Coun- cillor Leach asked if it was necessary to bury on Mon- day, and the doctor replied in the negative.—The Clerk (Mr. Brewis) : You must allow the relatives of the de- ceased an opportunity of burying the body if they wish, arid Monday is rather short time.—lnspector Walter Coates corroborated the doctor's statement, and the order was granted.

===== 0002601_18940421_art0178.txt | included=true | included =====
SHOCKING SUICIDE.

James Birch, sixty-three years of age, formerly a pig butcher, living alone at Wiedle-cottages, St. Helens, committed suicide on Monday. He had evidently cut his throat, and, feeling his weakness, had crawled to a sofa about five yards away and lain down upon it, and in that position he was found dead. He had not been seen since one o'clock on Sunday. A razor covered with blood was found on the table near. Police-sergeant Smith,. of Rainford, was called to the house, and he took possession of the razor. The deceased was a married man, but lived apart from his wife. A woman who goes to clean for him first made the discovery on Monday morning.—Mr. Brighouse, coroner, held an inquest at the Windleshaw Abbey Inn, St. Helens, on Wednesday, on the body of the deceased.— Thomas Birch, son of deceased, gave evidence of identi- fication. Ho last saw his father alive on Saturday, the 7th. He then appeared to be rambling, and was crying. and said he was a bit upset. He did not say anything more. His wife had not lived with him since Christmas and he had Neen living in the house alone.—The foreman of the jury said he saw the deceased a week previous, and he seemed all right then, with the exception of being a little depressed.—Mrs. Margaret Birch gave evidence as to finding Mr. Birch dead. He was in a sitting position on the sofa. She saw him on Friday night, and he then said he was quite a lunatic and did not know what he was doing. He left work a fortnight ago, and had been "drinking awful since."—bergeant Smith said he got information of the occurrence on Monday, and arrived at the house silently before one o'clock. The body did not appear to have been dis- turbed. On examining the body he found a wound in the throat about three inches across and very deep, apparently severing the windpipe. On going into the pantry, about five yards from where the body was, he • found a raz->r (produced) covered with blood. About a , fo. t from where the razor was lying there was a wash- ing mug with a large quantity of blood in it. The blood was clotted, and had been in some time. Witness saw Birch two or three times last week, and he was appar- ently drinking heavily.—The Foreman said they were of the opinion that he had feloniously killed himself and that there was not sufficient evidence to show that he was of unsound mind. That, the coroner said, was a verdict of Fete de se. -

ASSOCIATION MATCHES.

-- SEMI FINAL OF THE WARRINGTON AND DISTRICT CHALLENGE CUP.

WARRINGTON v. BARNTON ROVERS.—On Saturday, Barnton Rovers met Warrington at Dalton Bank. After some pretty even play, Bunton scored from a bully,and playing in a most determined fashion, brought half-time with the score unaltered. On restarting Warrington played up with spirit, and Norman shooting across the goal Mather breasted the ball through. Connor, after a grand run up the left, banged the leather through in splendid style. Barnton, not to be outdone, pressed for- ward, and Mather being slightly out of place the ball was shot through. A draw seemed practically certain when Connor, after another brilliant run, put on the winning goal, the game ended—Warrington, throe goals; Barnton, two. ST. ELPHIN'S T. Si'. HaLaria.—This match was played on the Cricket Ground, at Arpley, on Saturday. The game was of a rather interesting character, the I, eleven playing weakly, but at times the St. elens men showed excellent form. The final result was—St. Helens, two goals ; St. Elphin's, nil.

SHOCKINU ACCIDENT AT A ST. HELENS POTTERY.

Luke Houghton, a boy 13 years of age. and an apprentice, was killed on Thursday morning in the pottery of the St. Helens Brick Company. He was swing- ing on a belt when he was carried round a pulley and shafting to which it was attached, his head knocking against the ceiling at every revolution. His leg was alsi . twisted off in the drum. Death ensued half an hour after being released.

ANARCHISTS IN LONDON.

It is stated that Pohl, the Italian arrested on Saturday on the charge of being in unlawful possession of explo- sives, has given orders in London for several" infernal machines." On Tuesday the police ascertained that. a man believed to be Carnot, the comrade of Polti, had instructed an ironfounder in Lancaster-road to make a shell for the reception of explosives. Two detectives went to the works and found that the shell had been manufactured, and they removed it to Scotlaud Yard, where experts expressed the opinion that it vem tatedadact for an unlawful use.

A PENNY SAVED IS A PENNY MADE. How to save money. Waste not, want not. Don't furnish on the hire system, with its extravagant high price", arbitrary agreements, and despotic restrictions. For instance, suppose A furnishes for £I.QO on the hire system with B and pays B £99 in instalments, but neglects to pay B the last instalment in time, the agreement gives B the power to forcibly retake possession of the furniture, but the whole of the £93 paid is then t orfeited to B, consequently B gets both money and goods. In aVition to the alyfve disadvantages the price charged is exhorbitant, often 200 and 30 Tact- cent more than for cash at a proper establishment. It is high time this system was de lend illegal. Be it known that at Worsley's " The antheon," Gaythorn, Manchester, high-class New and Second hand Furniture may be had at the exact advertised marked cash price, no less under any circumstances. Also. that. in cases where it is inconvenient to pay fh whole of the money down, Worsley's are prepared to assist purchasers be allowing the account to be I aid at convenient intervals, charging only a small percentage for the privilege. By this means the purchaser knows exactly wh t he is charged for the goods and escapes all fear and trembling as to the loss of his furniture i he happens to miss a payment.
